On that set the functions inside the supremum and infimum converge uniformly. Their extrema therefore converge uniformly on the chosen parameter range. ◻

</details>

For a positive allowed length define the two slopes <a id="eq:envelope-slopes"></a>$$\label{eq:envelope-slopes}
  \mathcal{S}_\ell^+f(z)=\frac{\mathcal{P}_\ell f(z)-f(z)}{\ell},
  \qquad
  \mathcal{S}_\ell^-f(z)=\frac{f(z)-\mathcal{M}_\ell f(z)}{\ell}.$$ For a smooth function, the quantity $H_{\mathrm{sm}}=f_t+|\nabla_xf|^2/2$ satisfies $$F[f]=(\partial_t+\nabla_xf\cdot\nabla_x)H_{\mathrm{sm}}.$$ For the exact affine model $f(x,t)=p\cdot x+(a-|p|^2/2)t+c$ in <a href="#eq:limit-fit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-fit">[eq:limit-fit]</a>, one has $H_{\mathrm{sm}}=a$, and direct optimization gives $$\mathcal{P}_\ell f=f+a\ell,\qquad \mathcal{M}_\ell f=f-a\ell.$$ The optimizing displacements are respectively $\ell p$ and $-\ell p$. Thus both envelope slopes equal $a$, and both optimizer velocities, directed toward increasing time, equal $p$. These identifications concern the exact affine model; for a general fitted solution, $p,a$ remain the parameters of the chosen fit. For a general smooth function, a first-order expansion of the two quadratic optimizations formally gives $\mathcal{S}_\ell^\pm f=H_{\mathrm{sm}}+o(1)$ as $\ell\downarrow0$. For a smooth solution the identity for $F[f]$ transports this quantity along the direction $(\nabla_xf,1)$; its value may depend on the integral curve. The next lemma supplies the slope statements used for continuous solutions.

Evans and Savin <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[EvansSavin2008](#ref-EvansSavin2008), Theorem 3.1] proved the corresponding slope monotonicity and equality of the finite limits at a fixed center under their local boundedness and Lipschitz assumptions. The following formulation uses envelopes on the full spatial domain under subquadratic growth, separates the subsolution and supersolution conclusions, and allows the common limit to be $-\infty$.

<a id="stmt-lem-envelope-slopes"></a>
<div id="lem:envelope-slopes" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:subsolution-slope-order"></a>
<a id="eq:hamiltonian"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Envelope slopes). *For a subsolution $f$ as in Lemma <a href="#lem:quadratic-envelopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:quadratic-envelopes">16</a>, $\mathcal{S}_\ell^+f(z)$ is nondecreasing in $\ell$ and $$\label{eq:subsolution-slope-order}
  \limsup_{\ell\downarrow0}\mathcal{S}_\ell^-f(z)
       \le\lim_{\ell\downarrow0}\mathcal{S}_\ell^+f(z).$$ The right side may equal $-\infty$. For a supersolution, $\mathcal{S}_\ell^-f(z)$ is nondecreasing and $$\limsup_{\ell\downarrow0}\mathcal{S}_\ell^+f(z)
       \le\lim_{\ell\downarrow0}\mathcal{S}_\ell^-f(z).$$ Consequently, for a solution both slopes are nondecreasing and $$\label{eq:hamiltonian}
  \lim_{\ell\downarrow0}\mathcal{S}_\ell^+f(z)
  =\lim_{\ell\downarrow0}\mathcal{S}_\ell^-f(z)
  =:H_f(z)\in[-\infty,\infty).$$ The function $H_f$ is upper semicontinuous.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-envelope-slopes"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-envelope-slopes</summary>

*Proof.* Convexity of $\mathcal{P}_\ell f(z)$, with value $f(z)$ at zero, gives the first monotonicity. Let $\widetilde z_\ell=(y_\ell,t-\ell)$ be a backward optimizing point for $\mathcal{M}_\ell f(z)$. Using $z$ as a forward competitor from $\widetilde z_\ell$ gives $$\mathcal{S}_\ell^-f(z)\le\mathcal{S}_\ell^+f(\widetilde z_\ell).$$ The optimizer localization at length zero in Lemma <a href="#lem:quadratic-envelopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:quadratic-envelopes">16</a> shows $\widetilde z_\ell\to z$. Fix a small $h>0$ whose forward time argument is allowed near $z$. For all sufficiently small $\ell<h$, monotonicity bounds the right side by $\mathcal{S}_h^+f(\widetilde z_\ell)$. Continuity for fixed $h$ gives an upper limit at most $\mathcal{S}_h^+f(z)$. Now let $h\downarrow0$ to obtain <a href="#eq:subsolution-slope-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:subsolution-slope-order">[eq:subsolution-slope-order]</a>. This order of limits also covers the value $-\infty$.

Apply this conclusion to $\widetilde f(x,t)=-f(x,-t)$ to obtain the supersolution statements. For a solution both monotone limits exist and the two inequalities identify them. Each is bounded above by the finite slope at any fixed positive allowed length, proving the range in <a href="#eq:hamiltonian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hamiltonian">[eq:hamiltonian]</a>. Locally in $z$, choose a common small range of lengths. The common limit is the infimum of the continuous functions $\mathcal{S}_\ell^+f(z)$ over that range, so it is upper semicontinuous. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-fitted-envelope-bounds"></a>
<div id="lem:fitted-envelope-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:envelope-slope-bound"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Bounds for fitted solutions). *For a member $v\in\mathcal{A}_{m,C_0}$ centered at $(0,0)$ or $(0,1)$, $K\ge2$, $z\in\mathcal B_K$, and $0<\ell\le K$, one has $$\label{eq:envelope-slope-bound}
  \mathcal{S}_\ell^+v(z)\le C L(K)^4,\qquad
  \mathcal{S}_\ell^-v(z)\le C L(K)^4.$$ Every optimizing displacement for $\mathcal{P}_Kv(z)$ or $\mathcal{M}_Kv(z)$ has length at most $C K L(K)^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fitted-envelope-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fitted-envelope-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* By Lemma <a href="#lem:envelope-slopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:envelope-slopes">17</a>, it suffices to bound the two slopes at length $K$. Suppose an optimizing displacement has length $BK$ with $B\ge2$. Comparing its value with the competitor having no displacement, and using <a href="#eq:centered-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centered-growth">[eq:centered-growth]</a> in a box of size $(B+2)K$, gives $$\frac{B^2K}{2}
     \le C BK\bigl(L(K)+\log B\bigr)^2.$$ This estimate holds for the supremum and for the infimum. Consequently $B\le C(L(K)+\log B)^2\le2C L(K)^2+2C(\log B)^2$. Choose a fixed $B_0$ so large that $2C(\log B)^2\le B/2$ for $B\ge B_0$. The last inequality then yields $B\le4C L(K)^2$ for $B\ge B_0$, and the same type of bound holds when $B<B_0$. This proves the displacement claim. The optimizer and its time argument now lie in a box of size $C K L(K)^2$. Applying <a href="#eq:centered-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centered-growth">[eq:centered-growth]</a> there and observing that $L(C K L(K)^2)\le C L(K)$ bounds the absolute values of $v$ there by $C K L(K)^4$. Dropping the nonpositive cost in the expressions for the two slopes gives <a href="#eq:envelope-slope-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:envelope-slope-bound">[eq:envelope-slope-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

The criterion for the final limit is proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:slope-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:slope-rigidity">28</a>: if $v\in\mathcal{A}_{m,C_0}$ and there are $(b,t_0)\in\mathbb{R}^m\times\mathbb{R}$ and $B\in\mathbb{R}$ such that $$\mathcal{S}_\ell^+v(b,t_0)=\mathcal{S}_\ell^-v(b,t_0)=B
       \qquad\text{for every }\ell>0,$$ then $v$ is affine. The remaining argument will construct such a limit while retaining an obstruction to affinity.

<a id="sec:envelope-contact"></a>
### An extremal horizontal defect and contact

We next turn a nonaffine member of $\mathcal{A}_{m,C_0}$ into a contact between a solution and a subsolution. A finite horizontal probability configuration consists of positions $y_1,\ldots,y_N\in\mathbb{R}^m$ and weights $\theta_i>0$ with $\sum_i\theta_i=1$. Its mean and root mean square radius are $$x=\sum_i\theta_i y_i,\qquad
  r=\left(\sum_i\theta_i|y_i-x|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The barycentric envelope

<a id="eq:barycentric-envelope"></a>
Let $I\subseteq\mathbb{R}$ be an open interval. For a continuous function $f$ on $\mathbb{R}^m\times I$ with subquadratic spatial growth on compact time intervals, define, for $s>0$, $$\label{eq:barycentric-envelope}
  \mathcal{C}_s f(x,t)=
  \sup_{\substack{N,\ \theta_i>0,\ \sum_i\theta_i=1\\
                  \sum_i\theta_i y_i=x}}
   \left\{\sum_i\theta_i f(y_i,t)
             -\frac{\sum_i\theta_i|y_i-x|^2}{2s}\right\}.$$ The supremum is over finite configurations, including the single point $y_1=x$. This operation averages one horizontal slice at the fixed time $t$; $s$ controls the variance penalty. As the next lemma shows, $\mathcal{C}_s f(\cdot,t)-|\cdot|^2/(2s)$ is the least concave majorant of $f(\cdot,t)-|\cdot|^2/(2s)$.

<a id="eq:jensen-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:barycentric-sandwich"></a>
For the following estimate, suppose additionally that some $\kappa\ge0$ satisfies $$\label{eq:jensen-bound}
  \sum_i\theta_i f(y_i,t)-f(x,t)\le\kappa r$$ for every $t\in I$ and every finite horizontal probability configuration, with mean $x$ and radius $r$. Since $$\kappa r-\frac{r^2}{2s}
   =\frac{\kappa^2s}{2}-\frac{(r-\kappa s)^2}{2s},$$ the definition gives $$\label{eq:barycentric-sandwich}
  0\le\mathcal{C}_s f(x,t)-f(x,t)
     \le\sup_{r\ge0}\left(\kappa r-\frac{r^2}{2s}\right)
     =\frac{\kappa^2s}{2}.$$ The scalar maximum occurs at $r=\kappa s$. The extremal construction below supplies a solution $v$ and a positive $\kappa$ attaining this bound. We will prove that $\mathcal{C}_t v-\kappa^2t/2$ is a subsolution for $t>0$; the sandwich places it below $v$, with contact where equality holds and a strictly positive unshifted gap. The additional Jensen assumption applies only to this estimate; the next lemma uses only the stated continuity and growth hypotheses.

<a id="stmt-lem-barycentric-envelope"></a>
<div id="lem:barycentric-envelope" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:barycentric-duality"></a>
<a id="eq:barycentric-semiconcavity"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Barycentric envelope). *Let $f$ have the preceding growth property on $\mathbb{R}^m\times I$. For $s>0$ the supremum in <a href="#eq:barycentric-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-envelope">[eq:barycentric-envelope]</a> is attained by a finite configuration, and $$\label{eq:barycentric-duality}
  \mathcal{C}_s f(x,t)-\frac{|x|^2}{2s}
   =\min_{A\in\mathbb{R}^m}\ \max_{y\in\mathbb{R}^m}
       \left\{f(y,t)-\frac{|y|^2}{2s}-A\cdot(y-x)\right\}.$$ Both extrema on the right are attained. Allowing any probability measure of finite second moment and mean $x$ in <a href="#eq:barycentric-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-envelope">[eq:barycentric-envelope]</a> gives the same value. The function $\mathcal{C}_s f(x,t)$ is continuous in $(x,t,s)$ for $s>0$, and $$\label{eq:barycentric-semiconcavity}
  x\longmapsto\mathcal{C}_s f(x,t)-\frac{|x|^2}{2s}
       \quad\text{is concave}.$$ If $f_j\in C(\mathbb{R}^m\times I)$, $f_j\to f$ locally uniformly, and the sequence has the common growth control $$\lim_{M\to\infty}
  \sup_j\sup_{\substack{|y|\ge M\\t\in J}}
       \frac{|f_j(y,t)|}{|y|^2}=0
       \qquad(J\Subset I),$$ then $\mathcal{C}_s f_j\to\mathcal{C}_s f$ locally uniformly for $s>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-barycentric-envelope"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-barycentric-envelope</summary>

*Proof.* For fixed $(t,s)$ put $g(y)=f(y,t)-|y|^2/(2s)$ and $$\Gamma(A;x,t,s)=\sup_y\{g(y)-A\cdot(y-x)\}.$$ The expression on the left of <a href="#eq:barycentric-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-duality">[eq:barycentric-duality]</a> is the supremum of $\sum_i\theta_i g(y_i)$ over configurations of mean $x$, because $\sum_i\theta_i|y_i-x|^2=\sum_i\theta_i|y_i|^2-|x|^2$. It is therefore at most $\inf_A\Gamma(A;x,t,s)$.

Fix a compact range of $(x,t,s)$ with $s>0$. For $A\ne0$, use $y=x-A/|A|$ in the supremum to get $\Gamma(A;x,t,s)\ge |A|-C$ on this range. The value at $A=0$ is uniformly bounded above, since the negative quadratic part of $g$ dominates the subquadratic growth of $f$. Thus every minimizing sequence whose values are at most $\Gamma(0)+1$ lies in a fixed bounded set of slopes. For slopes in that set, the quantity $g(y)-A\cdot(y-x)$ tends uniformly to $-\infty$ as $|y|\to\infty$. Its maximizing positions therefore lie in one compact set. It follows that $\Gamma$ is continuous on the relevant set, the minimum and maxima are attained, and their value depends continuously on $(x,t,s)$.

Let $A_0$ be a minimizing slope, and let $E$ be its nonempty compact set of maximizing positions. Then $x$ belongs to the convex hull of $E$. Otherwise strict separation gives a vector $e$ and $\delta>0$ with $e\cdot(y-x)\ge\delta$ for all $y\in E$. If $y_h$ maximizes $\Gamma(A_0+he;x,t,s)$ for $h>0$, the uniform compactness of maximizers and continuity show that every limit of $y_h$ as $h\downarrow0$ belongs to $E$. For small $h$, $e\cdot(y_h-x)\ge\delta/2$, and hence $$\Gamma(A_0+he;x,t,s)
   \le\Gamma(A_0;x,t,s)-h\delta/2,$$ contradicting minimality. The finite-dimensional convex hull theorem now represents $x$ as a convex combination of finitely many points of $E$. Discard any zero weights. Their weighted values of $g$ equal $\Gamma(A_0;x,t,s)$, proving the reverse inequality in <a href="#eq:barycentric-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-duality">[eq:barycentric-duality]</a> and finite attainment in <a href="#eq:barycentric-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-envelope">[eq:barycentric-envelope]</a>.

The affine majorant $g(y)\le\Gamma(A_0;x,t,s)+A_0\cdot(y-x)$ can also be integrated against any probability measure of mean $x$ and finite second moment. All terms are integrable by the growth hypothesis. Thus such measures cannot increase the value. The right side of <a href="#eq:barycentric-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-duality">[eq:barycentric-duality]</a> is an infimum of affine functions of $x$, so it is concave in $x$. This is <a href="#eq:barycentric-semiconcavity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-semiconcavity">[eq:barycentric-semiconcavity]</a>.

For the convergence assertion, on each compact range of $(x,t,s)$ the same preceding bounds on slopes and maximizing positions hold uniformly for $f_j$: local uniform convergence gives common bounds near the compact range, and the common subquadratic control handles large $y$. Both extrema in <a href="#eq:barycentric-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-duality">[eq:barycentric-duality]</a> can therefore be restricted to fixed compact sets. On these sets the expressions converge uniformly, proving the asserted local uniform convergence. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### A common-time subsolution rule

To test the barycentric envelope, we must combine subsolution inequalities at several spatial points sharing one time variable. Lemma <a href="#lem:sum-rule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sum-rule">6</a> is applied after separating and then synchronizing those times; the penalty Hessian vanishes on the direction with time speed $1$ in every component, which is the direction used by $F$.

<a id="stmt-lem-common-time-subsolution"></a>
<div id="lem:common-time-subsolution" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:common-time-inequality"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Common-time subsolutions). *Let $v$ be a continuous viscosity subsolution of <a href="#eq:limit-pde" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-pde">[eq:limit-pde]</a> on $\mathbb{R}^m\times I$, and fix positive weights $\theta_i$ with $\sum_{i=1}^N\theta_i=1$. Define $$V((y_i)_{i=1}^N,t)=\sum_{i=1}^N\theta_i v(y_i,t).$$ If a $C^2$ function $\Phi$ touches $V$ from above at $(Y_0,t_0)$, where $Y=(y_i)_{i=1}^N$, then $$\label{eq:common-time-inequality}
  D^2\Phi(Y_0,t_0)
   \left[\left(
       \bigl(\theta_i^{-1}\nabla_{y_i}\Phi(Y_0,t_0)\bigr)_{i=1}^N,
       1\right)\right]\ge0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-common-time-subsolution"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-common-time-subsolution</summary>

*Proof.* Replace $\Phi$ by $\Phi^\sharp=\Phi+|Y-Y_0|^4+|t-t_0|^4$. This makes the local maximum strict while preserving the derivatives through order two at the contact. Choose a small closed product neighborhood of $((y_i^0,t_0))_{i=1}^N$, contained in the domain, such that the diagonal points $(Y,t)$ lie in this strict neighborhood. For independent time variables $T=(t_1,\ldots,t_N)$, put $$\bar t=\frac1N\sum_i t_i,\qquad
  \chi(T)=\frac12\sum_i(t_i-\bar t)^2,\qquad
  \Psi_\lambda(Y,T)=\Phi^\sharp(Y,\bar t)+\lambda\chi(T).$$ Maximize $\sum_i\theta_i v(y_i,t_i)-\Psi_\lambda(Y,T)$ on the closed product. Comparison with the original diagonal point and boundedness on this product show $\lambda\chi(T_\lambda)\le C$ at any maximum. Thus all time deviations tend to zero as $\lambda\to\infty$. Every limit of such maximum points is diagonal; dropping the nonnegative penalty in the maximum inequality shows that its diagonal limit is a maximum of $V-\Phi^\sharp$. Strictness forces this limit to be $(Y_0,t_0)$. Hence all the maximum points converge to the original point and are interior for sufficiently large $\lambda$.

Fix one such $\lambda$ and one maximum. Set $$q_i=\theta_i^{-1}\nabla_{y_i}
             \Phi^\sharp(Y_\lambda,\bar t_\lambda),
  \qquad
  \zeta_\lambda=((q_i,1))_{i=1}^N,\qquad
  A_\lambda=D^2\Psi_\lambda(Y_\lambda,T_\lambda),$$ where $A_\lambda$ is written in the separate variables $((y_i,t_i))_i$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:sum-rule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sum-rule">6</a> to the functions $\theta_i v(y_i,t_i)$ and the test $\Psi_\lambda$. For every $\eta>0$ it supplies closed superjets with Hessians $X_i$ and first derivatives $D_{(y_i,t_i)}\Psi_\lambda$, with $\operatorname{diag}(X_i)\le A_\lambda+\eta A_\lambda^2$. Dividing each jet by the positive number $\theta_i$ gives a closed superjet of $v$ with horizontal gradient $q_i$. The viscosity inequalities therefore give $X_i[(q_i,1)]\ge0$. Evaluating the matrix inequality on $\zeta_\lambda$ yields $$0\le\sum_iX_i[(q_i,1)]
     \le A_\lambda[\zeta_\lambda]
                   +\eta|A_\lambda\zeta_\lambda|^2.$$ First let $\eta\downarrow0$, keeping $\lambda$ and its maximum fixed. The quantities $A_\lambda$ and $\zeta_\lambda$ do not depend on $\eta$, so $A_\lambda[\zeta_\lambda]\ge0$. The time component of $\zeta_\lambda$ is $(1,\ldots,1)$, which is in the kernel of $D^2\chi$. The linear map $(Y,T)\mapsto(Y,\bar t)$ sends $\zeta_\lambda$ to $((q_i)_i,1)$. We conclude that $$D^2\Phi^\sharp(Y_\lambda,\bar t_\lambda)
       [((q_i)_i,1)]\ge0.$$ Now let $\lambda\to\infty$. The maximum points converge to the contact, and the horizontal derivatives converge there. The derivatives of $\Phi^\sharp$ at that point are those of $\Phi$, giving <a href="#eq:common-time-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:common-time-inequality">[eq:common-time-inequality]</a>. The time components of the first derivatives of $\Psi_\lambda$ need no bound: they do not enter the direction $(\nabla_xv,1)$ in the individual viscosity inequalities. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-barycentric-subsolution"></a>
<div id="cor:barycentric-subsolution" class="corollary">

**Corollary 21** (A barycentric subsolution). *Let $f\in C(\mathbb{R}^m\times(0,\infty))$ be a viscosity subsolution of <a href="#eq:limit-pde" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-pde">[eq:limit-pde]</a> with subquadratic spatial growth on compact time intervals. Then $(x,t)\mapsto\mathcal{C}_t f(x,t)$ is a viscosity subsolution of <a href="#eq:limit-pde" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-pde">[eq:limit-pde]</a> on $\mathbb{R}^m\times(0,\infty)$. For every $c\in\mathbb{R}$, the same holds for $(x,t)\mapsto\mathcal{C}_t f(x,t)-ct$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-barycentric-subsolution"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-barycentric-subsolution</summary>

*Proof.* Continuity follows from Lemma <a href="#lem:barycentric-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:barycentric-envelope">19</a>. Fix $c\in\mathbb{R}$, and let $\phi$ touch $(x,t)\mapsto\mathcal{C}_t f(x,t)-ct$ from above at $(x_0,t_0)$ with $t_0>0$. Choose an attaining finite configuration $(\theta_i,y_i^0)$ for $\mathcal{C}_{t_0}f(x_0,t_0)$, and keep its positive weights fixed. For variable $Y=(y_i)_i$ let $X(Y)=\sum_i\theta_i y_i$ and define $$\Phi(Y,t)=\phi(X(Y),t)+ct
              +\frac{\sum_i\theta_i|y_i-X(Y)|^2}{2t}.$$ The defining supremum for $\mathcal{C}_t$ shows that $\sum_i\theta_i f(y_i,t)-\Phi(Y,t)$ has a local maximum at $(Y_0,t_0)$: for nearby $(Y,t)$ it is at most $\mathcal{C}_t f(X(Y),t)-ct-\phi(X(Y),t)$, and equality holds at the chosen configuration. Set $p=\nabla_x\phi(x_0,t_0)$. The individual horizontal directions in Lemma <a href="#lem:common-time-subsolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:common-time-subsolution">20</a> are $$q_i=\theta_i^{-1}\nabla_{y_i}\Phi
       =p+\frac{y_i^0-x_0}{t_0}.$$ Their weighted mean is $p$. Along the straight motion with velocities $y_i'=q_i$ and $t'=1$, the mean has velocity $p$ and each offset $y_i-X$ has velocity $(y_i^0-x_0)/t_0$. Consequently, along this motion the variance term is $$\frac{\sum_i\theta_i
           |(y_i^0-x_0)(1+h/t_0)|^2}{2(t_0+h)}
    =\frac{t_0+h}{2t_0^2}
           \sum_i\theta_i|y_i^0-x_0|^2,$$ which is affine in $h$. The term $ct$ is also affine, and the Hessian of the remaining term along this motion is $D^2\phi[(p,1)]$. Lemma <a href="#lem:common-time-subsolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:common-time-subsolution">20</a>, applied to the subsolution inequality for $f$, therefore gives $F[\phi](x_0,t_0)\ge0$. This proves the claim for every $c$, including $c=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Construction and slope of the contact

<a id="stmt-prop-envelope-contact"></a>
<div id="prop:envelope-contact" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:contact-envelope"></a>
<a id="eq:contact-sandwich"></a>
<a id="eq:contact-barycentric-gap"></a>
<a id="eq:contact-slope"></a>
**Proposition 22** (Extremal envelope contact). *If $\mathcal{A}_{m,C_0}$ contains a nonaffine member, then there exist $\kappa\in(0,\infty)$ and $v\in\mathcal{A}_{m,C_0}$ centered at $z_*=(0,1)$ for which <a href="#eq:jensen-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jensen-bound">[eq:jensen-bound]</a> holds with $f=v$ and $I=\mathbb{R}$. For $t>0$ set $$\label{eq:contact-envelope}
  W(x,t)=\mathcal{C}_t v(x,t)-\frac{\kappa^2t}{2}.$$ This function is continuous, has subquadratic spatial growth on compact positive-time intervals, and is a viscosity subsolution of <a href="#eq:limit-pde" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-pde">[eq:limit-pde]</a> on $\mathbb{R}^m\times(0,\infty)$. It satisfies $$\label{eq:contact-sandwich}
  v(x,t)-\frac{\kappa^2t}{2}\le W(x,t)\le v(x,t)
       \quad(t>0),\qquad W(z_*)=v(z_*)=0,$$ and $W(\cdot,t)-|x|^2/(2t)$ is concave for every $t>0$. At every point of contact with $t>0$, the barycentric gap satisfies $$\label{eq:contact-barycentric-gap}
  W(x,t)=v(x,t)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  \frac{\mathcal{C}_t v(x,t)-v(x,t)}{t}=\frac{\kappa^2}{2}>0.$$ Finally, $$\label{eq:contact-slope}
  H_v(z_*)\in\mathbb{R},\qquad
  \lim_{\ell\downarrow0}\mathcal{S}_\ell^+W(z_*)=H_v(z_*).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-envelope-contact"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-envelope-contact</summary>

*Proof.* *A positive finite horizontal defect.* Start with a nonaffine member $v^{(0)}$ and center it at the origin using Lemma <a href="#lem:frame-invariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frame-invariance">14</a>. Some horizontal slice of $v^{(0)}$ is nonaffine. To verify this, suppose instead that $v^{(0)}(x,t)=p(t)\cdot x+c(t)$ for every $t$. The coefficients $p,c$ are continuous, as follows by evaluating the function at $0$ and at the coordinate vectors. Direct optimization gives $$\mathcal{P}_\ell v^{(0)}(x,t)
   =p(t+\ell)\cdot x+c(t+\ell)
                         +\frac{\ell}{2}|p(t+\ell)|^2.$$ For every fixed $x,t$ this is convex in $\ell$. In each convexity inequality, the coefficient of $x$ must vanish, since the inequality holds for all $x\in\mathbb{R}^m$. Hence $p$ satisfies the affine interpolation identity on every interval, and so is affine in $t$. On the other hand, <a href="#eq:centered-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centered-growth">[eq:centered-growth]</a>, applied at $x=\pm Ke$ and $|t|\le K$, implies $|p(t)|\le C L(K)^2$. An affine function of $t$ with this bound as $K\to\infty$ is constant. For constant $p$, the displayed forward formula and its backward counterpart $$\mathcal{M}_\ell v^{(0)}(x,t)
   =p\cdot x+c(t-\ell)-\frac{\ell}{2}|p|^2$$ show that $c$ is both convex and concave. It is therefore affine, contradicting the choice of $v^{(0)}$.

<a id="eq:jensen-supremum"></a>
A continuous nonaffine spatial slice has a nonzero midpoint Jensen defect, since the midpoint identity everywhere would make that slice affine. The transformation $f(x,t)\mapsto-f(x,-t)$ preserves $\mathcal{A}_{m,C_0}$: it exchanges the two viscosity inequalities, and it sends a fit $(p,a)$ to $(-p,a)$. It also changes the sign of a horizontal Jensen defect. Apply this transformation if needed, so that $v^{(0)}$ has a positive defect. Define $$\label{eq:jensen-supremum}
  \kappa=\sup_{\substack{t,\ \text{finite configurations}\\r>0}}
     \frac{\sum_i\theta_i v^{(0)}(y_i,t)-v^{(0)}(x,t)}{r}.$$ Then $\kappa>0$. It is finite as well. For any configuration in this supremum, choose a fit at its mean $(x,t)$ of radius $r$ and use <a href="#eq:frame-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frame-change">[eq:frame-change]</a> with these data. At time zero the new positions are $(y_i-x)/r$, with mean zero and second moment one. The defect divided by $r$ is unchanged, since the affine terms cancel in the weighted difference. The new function is centered. Its growth bound implies $|\bar v(y,0)|\le C(1+|y|^2)$, with one constant for all these normalizations. Integrating this inequality over a configuration of second moment one bounds the quotient by a fixed constant. Thus $0<\kappa<\infty$. More generally, <a href="#eq:frame-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frame-change">[eq:frame-change]</a> preserves every such quotient, at every time: spatial differences and their root mean square radius both scale by $R$, and the subtracted affine terms cancel.

*Attainment after compactness.* Choose configurations whose quotients in <a href="#eq:jensen-supremum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jensen-supremum">[eq:jensen-supremum]</a> tend to $\kappa$, and for each one make the normalization just described. Denote the resulting functions by $v_j$ and the normalized finite probability measures by $\mu_j$. Then $$v_j(0,0)=0,\qquad
  \int y\,d\mu_j=0,\qquad
  \int |y|^2\,d\mu_j=1,\qquad
  \int v_j(y,0)\,d\mu_j\longrightarrow\kappa.$$ Every $v_j$ is centered at the origin, and every finite configuration for it satisfies the Jensen bound with constant $\kappa$. First use Lemma <a href="#lem:fit-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fit-compactness">15</a> to pass to a locally uniform limit $\widehat v\in\mathcal{A}_{m,C_0}$, centered at the origin. The Jensen bound passes to this limit for each fixed finite configuration.

<a id="eq:extremal-measure"></a>
The moment bound gives $\mu_j(\{|y|>M\})\le M^{-2}$, so the measures are tight. After a further subsequence they converge weakly to a probability measure $\mu$. Lower semicontinuity of the second moment gives $\int|y|^2\,d\mu\le1$. The mean remains zero: the bound $\int_{|y|>M}|y|\,d\mu_j\le M^{-1}$ makes the first moments uniformly integrable, and the same bound holds for the limit. To retain the extremal defect, we also need convergence of the value integrals despite the varying functions and measures. For a compactly supported continuous cutoff $\chi_M$, local uniform convergence and weak convergence give $$\int\chi_M(y)v_j(y,0)\,d\mu_j
       \longrightarrow
  \int\chi_M(y)\widehat v(y,0)\,d\mu.$$ Choose $\chi_M=1$ on $\{|y|\le M\}$ and zero outside $\{|y|<2M\}$. By <a href="#eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth">[eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth]</a>, $$\int_{|y|>M}|v_j(y,0)|\,d\mu_j
  \le
  \sup_{\substack{j\\|y|>M}}\frac{|v_j(y,0)|}{|y|^2}
       \int|y|^2\,d\mu_j
  \longrightarrow0$$ uniformly in $j$; the analogous tail bound holds for $\widehat v$ and $\mu$. Letting first $j\to\infty$ and then $M\to\infty$ proves $$\label{eq:extremal-measure}
  \int y\,d\mu=0,\qquad
  \int|y|^2\,d\mu\le1,\qquad
  \int\widehat v(y,0)\,d\mu-\widehat v(0,0)=\kappa.$$

Apply <a href="#eq:barycentric-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-sandwich">[eq:barycentric-sandwich]</a> to $\widehat v$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:barycentric-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:barycentric-envelope">19</a>, the measure in <a href="#eq:extremal-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extremal-measure">[eq:extremal-measure]</a> is admissible for the value of $\mathcal{C}_{1/\kappa}\widehat v(0,0)$. It gives $$\mathcal{C}_{1/\kappa}\widehat v(0,0)-\widehat v(0,0)
     \ge\kappa-\frac{\kappa}{2}\int|y|^2\,d\mu
     \ge\frac{\kappa}{2}.$$ This is equality by <a href="#eq:barycentric-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-sandwich">[eq:barycentric-sandwich]</a>. In particular, loss of second moment in the weak limit causes no loss of contact; the equality also forces $\int|y|^2\,d\mu=1$.

<a id="eq:normalized-envelope-contact"></a>
Set $R=1/\kappa$, and choose a fit $(p_0,a_0)$ for $\widehat v$ at $(0,0)$ of radius $R$. Define the final function by the single change of frame and time translation $$v(y,t)=
   \frac{\widehat v(R(y+p_0(t-1)),R(t-1))-\widehat v(0,0)}{R}
       -p_0\cdot y
       -\left(a_0+\frac{|p_0|^2}{2}\right)(t-1).$$ It is centered at $(0,1)$ and still has the global Jensen bound with constant $\kappa$. Under the change of frame <a href="#eq:frame-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frame-change">[eq:frame-change]</a>, at corresponding points $Z$ and $(y,s)$ the envelope gaps obey $$\mathcal{C}_\sigma\bar v(y,s)-\bar v(y,s)
     =\frac{\mathcal{C}_{R\sigma}\widehat v(Z)-\widehat v(Z)}{R}.$$ This follows by scaling the spatial variance by $R^2$ in <a href="#eq:barycentric-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-envelope">[eq:barycentric-envelope]</a>; the affine terms again cancel. The preceding equality at parameter $1/\kappa=R$ therefore becomes $$\label{eq:normalized-envelope-contact}
  \mathcal{C}_1v(0,1)-v(0,1)=\frac{\kappa^2}{2}.$$

*The subsolution below $v$.* Define $W$ by <a href="#eq:contact-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-envelope">[eq:contact-envelope]</a>. Equations <a href="#eq:barycentric-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-sandwich">[eq:barycentric-sandwich]</a> and <a href="#eq:normalized-envelope-contact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-envelope-contact">[eq:normalized-envelope-contact]</a> give <a href="#eq:contact-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-sandwich">[eq:contact-sandwich]</a>. At any contact, <a href="#eq:contact-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-envelope">[eq:contact-envelope]</a> also gives <a href="#eq:contact-barycentric-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-barycentric-gap">[eq:contact-barycentric-gap]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:barycentric-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:barycentric-envelope">19</a> gives continuity and the stated spatial semiconcavity. The sandwich and <a href="#eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth">[eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth]</a> give subquadratic spatial growth on every compact positive-time interval. Since $v$ is a solution with the growth in <a href="#eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth">[eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth]</a>, Corollary <a href="#cor:barycentric-subsolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:barycentric-subsolution">21</a>, with $f=v$ and $c=\kappa^2/2$, gives the subsolution property of $W$.

<a id="eq:contact-configuration"></a>
*A finite slope at the contact.* Lemma <a href="#lem:envelope-slopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:envelope-slopes">17</a> already bounds $H_v$ above at every point. We first bound it below at the finitely many points supporting the extremal configuration, using one common forward paraboloid, and then transfer this bound to their barycenter through equality in the Jensen bound. Choose a finite attaining configuration in <a href="#eq:normalized-envelope-contact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-envelope-contact">[eq:normalized-envelope-contact]</a>, with mean $x=0$, radius $r$, and positive weights. If $D=\sum_i\theta_i v(y_i,1)-v(0,1)$, its attainment and the Jensen bound imply $$\frac{\kappa^2}{2}=D-\frac{r^2}{2}
       \le\kappa r-\frac{r^2}{2}
       =\frac{\kappa^2}{2}-\frac{(r-\kappa)^2}{2}.$$ All inequalities are equalities, so $$\label{eq:contact-configuration}
  r=\kappa,\qquad
  \sum_i\theta_i v(y_i,1)-v(0,1)=\kappa r.$$ Let $A$ be a minimizing slope in <a href="#eq:barycentric-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-duality">[eq:barycentric-duality]</a> for $(x,t,s)=(0,1,1)$. The weighted attaining value equals its affine majorant. Since all weights are positive, each $y_i$ must attain that majorant. The identity $$v(y,1)-\frac{|y|^2}{2}-A\cdot y
   =v(y,1)-\frac{|y+A|^2}{2}+\frac{|A|^2}{2}$$ therefore shows that all $y_i$ are optimizers for $\mathcal{P}_1v(b,0)$ with $b=-A$.

Put $h(s)=\mathcal{P}_s v(b,0)$ for $0\le s\le1$. It is finite and convex by Lemma <a href="#lem:quadratic-envelopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:quadratic-envelopes">16</a>. For $0<\ell<1$, use the point $y_{i,\ell}^-=b+(1-\ell)(y_i-b)$ at time $1-\ell$ as a competitor for $\mathcal{M}_\ell v(y_i,1)$. Its value is bounded above using $\mathcal{P}_{1-\ell}v(b,0)$. The two quadratic costs on this linear path add, giving explicitly $$\mathcal{M}_\ell v(y_i,1)
      \le h(1-\ell)+\frac{|y_i-b|^2}{2},
  \qquad
  v(y_i,1)=h(1)+\frac{|y_i-b|^2}{2}.$$ It follows that $$\mathcal{S}_\ell^-v(y_i,1)
     \ge\frac{h(1)-h(1-\ell)}{\ell}
     \ge h(1)-h(0).$$ The last inequality is the chord inequality for the convex function $h$. Taking the slope limit shows $H_v(y_i,1)\ge h(1)-h(0)>-\infty$. These finitely many values are also finite above by Lemma <a href="#lem:envelope-slopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:envelope-slopes">17</a>.

For each $\ell>0$, choose an optimizer $\widehat y_i$ for $\mathcal{P}_\ell v(y_i,1)$ and let $\widehat x=\sum_i\theta_i\widehat y_i$. Write $\xi_i=y_i-x$, $\widehat\xi_i=\widehat y_i-\widehat x$, and $r'=(\sum_i\theta_i|\widehat\xi_i|^2)^{1/2}$. The exact orthogonal decomposition and the reverse triangle inequality in the weighted Euclidean space give $$\sum_i\theta_i|\widehat y_i-y_i|^2
    =|\widehat x-x|^2+
        \sum_i\theta_i|\widehat\xi_i-\xi_i|^2
    \ge|\widehat x-x|^2+(r'-r)^2.$$ Use the Jensen bound at time $1+\ell$, <a href="#eq:contact-configuration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-configuration">[eq:contact-configuration]</a> at time $1$, and $\widehat x$ as a forward competitor from $x$. The decomposition yields $$\begin{aligned}
  \ell\sum_i\theta_i\mathcal{S}_\ell^+v(y_i,1)
   &\le \ell\mathcal{S}_\ell^+v(x,1)
       +\kappa(r'-r)-\frac{(r'-r)^2}{2\ell}\\
   &\le \ell\mathcal{S}_\ell^+v(x,1)+\frac{\kappa^2\ell}{2}.
\end{aligned}$$ Divide by $\ell$ and take $\ell\downarrow0$. There are only finitely many endpoints, all with finite limits, so $$H_v(z_*)\ge
     \sum_i\theta_i H_v(y_i,1)-\frac{\kappa^2}{2}
     \ge h(1)-h(0)-\frac{\kappa^2}{2}>-\infty.$$ Its upper finiteness is part of Lemma <a href="#lem:envelope-slopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:envelope-slopes">17</a>; hence $H_v(z_*)\in\mathbb{R}$.

It remains to identify the forward slope of $W$. At the contact, the sandwich gives for every $\ell>0$ $\mathcal{S}_\ell^+W(z_*)\le\mathcal{S}_\ell^+v(z_*)$. For $0<\ell<1$ it also gives $\mathcal{S}_\ell^-W(z_*)\ge\mathcal{S}_\ell^-v(z_*)$, since the backward time is positive. The subsolution property and growth of $W$ allow the one-sided inequality <a href="#eq:subsolution-slope-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:subsolution-slope-order">[eq:subsolution-slope-order]</a> to be applied to it. Consequently $$H_v(z_*)\le
  \limsup_{\ell\downarrow0}\mathcal{S}_\ell^-W(z_*)
  \le\lim_{\ell\downarrow0}\mathcal{S}_\ell^+W(z_*)
  \le H_v(z_*).$$ This proves <a href="#eq:contact-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-slope">[eq:contact-slope]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:discrete-propagation"></a>
### Discrete propagation inequalities

We now derive the one-step inequality used to propagate the contact from Proposition <a href="#prop:envelope-contact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:envelope-contact">22</a>. The argument will also be applied after normalizing at fitted points that need not be contacts, so the estimates must hold without assuming contact at the center. Stopped stochastic processes and finite-difference comparisons have also been used to study the infinity Laplacian <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[PeresSchrammSheffieldWilson2009](#ref-PeresSchrammSheffieldWilson2009), [ArmstrongSmart2010](#ref-ArmstrongSmart2010)]. The walk constructed here uses forward and backward quadratic optimizers for the limiting equation; its drift controls velocity discrepancies that persist under fitted changes of frame.

Throughout this section, let $v\in\mathcal{A}_{m,C_0}$ be centered at $(0,1)$, and fix $0\le\kappa<\infty$ for which <a href="#eq:jensen-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jensen-bound">[eq:jensen-bound]</a> holds with $f=v$ and $I=\mathbb{R}$. For $t>0$, define $$W(x,t)=\mathcal{C}_t v(x,t)-\frac{\kappa^2t}{2}.$$ The general bound <a href="#eq:barycentric-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:barycentric-sandwich">[eq:barycentric-sandwich]</a>, with its parameter equal to $t$, gives $$v(x,t)-\frac{\kappa^2t}{2}\le W(x,t)\le v(x,t).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:barycentric-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:barycentric-envelope">19</a> gives continuity of $W$ and concavity of $W(\cdot,t)-|\cdot|^2/(2t)$. The sandwich and <a href="#eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth">[eq:uniform-subquadratic-growth]</a> give subquadratic spatial growth for $W$ on compact positive-time intervals. Since $v$ is a subsolution with that growth, Corollary <a href="#cor:barycentric-subsolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:barycentric-subsolution">21</a>, applied with $c=\kappa^2/2$, makes $W$ a subsolution of <a href="#eq:limit-pde" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-pde">[eq:limit-pde]</a>.

<a id="eq:dp-contact-gap"></a>
<a id="eq:dp-J-bounds"></a>
For $\ell,\epsilon>0$, set <a id="eq:dp-auxiliary"></a>$$\label{eq:dp-auxiliary}
  f=W,\qquad f^\ell=\mathcal{P}_\ell f,\qquad
  g_\epsilon=\mathcal{M}_\epsilon v,\qquad
  J_\epsilon=\frac{f^\epsilon-g_\epsilon}{2\epsilon}.$$ These functions are defined at every positive time. The identity $$\label{eq:dp-contact-gap}
\begin{split}
  2\epsilon J_\epsilon(z)
  &=W(z)-v(z)+\bigl(\mathcal{P}_\epsilon W(z)-W(z)\bigr)\\
  &\hspace{4em}+\bigl(v(z)-\mathcal{M}_\epsilon v(z)\bigr)
\end{split}$$ and the zero-length continuity in Lemma <a href="#lem:quadratic-envelopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:quadratic-envelopes">16</a> show that $2\epsilon J_\epsilon\to W-v$ uniformly on every compact subset of $\mathbb{R}^m\times(0,\infty)$. Thus, on a compact region separated from contact, $J_\epsilon$ tends uniformly to $-\infty$. This fact will turn lower expectation bounds for $J_\epsilon$ into contact at limiting marks. The sandwich also gives $\mathcal{P}_\epsilon W\le\mathcal{P}_\epsilon v$, and hence $$\label{eq:dp-J-bounds}
  J_\epsilon(z)\le
  \frac12\bigl(\mathcal{S}^+_\epsilon v(z)+\mathcal{S}^-_\epsilon v(z)\bigr).$$

<a id="eq:dp-J-contact"></a>
For the particular pair supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:envelope-contact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:envelope-contact">22</a>, write $z_*=(0,1)$ and $H_*=H_v(z_*)\in\mathbb{R}$. Equality of $W$ and $v$ there, the contact slope conclusion, and the common slope limit for $v$ give $$\label{eq:dp-J-contact}
  J_\epsilon(z_*)
  =\frac12\bigl(\mathcal{S}^+_\epsilon W(z_*)+
                    \mathcal{S}^-_\epsilon v(z_*)\bigr)
  \longrightarrow H_*\qquad(\epsilon\downarrow0).$$ This finite limiting value will start the walks in Section <a href="#sec:contact-localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:contact-localization">7</a>.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Spatial regularity and the one-step drift

<a id="stmt-lem-dp-spatial-regularity"></a>
<div id="lem:dp-spatial-regularity" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:dp-gradient"></a>
<a id="eq:dp-supports"></a>
**Lemma 23**. *For every $t,\ell>0$, the optimization defining $f^\ell(x,t)$ has a unique maximizer $y=y(x,t,\ell)$. The function $f^\ell(\cdot,t)$ is differentiable, and $$\label{eq:dp-gradient}
  \nabla_x f^\ell(x,t)=\frac{y-x}{\ell}.$$ For every $h\in\mathbb{R}^m$ it satisfies the two supporting inequalities $$\label{eq:dp-supports}
\begin{split}
  f^\ell(x,t)+\nabla_x f^\ell(x,t)\cdot h-\frac{|h|^2}{2\ell}
  &\le f^\ell(x+h,t)\\
  &\le f^\ell(x,t)+\nabla_x f^\ell(x,t)\cdot h+\frac{|h|^2}{2t}.
\end{split}$$ In particular, its spatial Hessian is bounded below by $-\ell^{-1}I$ in the semiconvexity sense and above by $t^{-1}I$ in the semiconcavity sense.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dp-spatial-regularity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dp-spatial-regularity</summary>

<a id="eq:dp-lower-support"></a>
*Proof.* Put $s=t+\ell$. The function $f(\cdot,s)-|\cdot|^2/(2s)$ is concave, because the same is true of $\mathcal{C}_s v(\cdot,s)-|\cdot|^2/(2s)$. Thus $$y\longmapsto f(y,s)-\frac{|y-x|^2}{2\ell}$$ is strictly concave: after subtracting the concave function $f(y,s)-|y|^2/(2s)$, its quadratic part has Hessian $(s^{-1}-\ell^{-1})I=-t(\ell s)^{-1}I$. It has a maximizer by the subquadratic growth and the quadratic cost, and strict concavity makes the maximizer unique. Keeping this maximizer fixed when $x$ is replaced by $x+h$ gives $$\label{eq:dp-lower-support}
  f^\ell(x+h,t)\ge f^\ell(x,t)+\frac{y-x}{\ell}\cdot h
                                      -\frac{|h|^2}{2\ell}.$$

For the upper bound, the identity $$\frac{|y|^2}{2s}-\frac{|y-x|^2}{2\ell}-\frac{|x|^2}{2t}
  =-\frac{t}{2\ell s}\left|y-\frac{s}{t}x\right|^2$$ shows that $$f^\ell(x,t)-\frac{|x|^2}{2t}
  =\sup_y\left\{f(y,s)-\frac{|y|^2}{2s}
                  -\frac{t}{2\ell s}\left|y-\frac{s}{t}x\right|^2\right\}$$ is concave in $x$. Indeed, the expression in braces is jointly concave in $(x,y)$; applying that concavity to a pair of maximizing points proves concavity of its partial supremum. A finite concave function on $\mathbb{R}^m$ has an upper supporting affine function at each point. Adding back the quadratic therefore gives a vector $q$ such that $$f^\ell(x+h,t)\le f^\ell(x,t)+q\cdot h+\frac{|h|^2}{2t}
  \quad\text{for every }h.$$ Comparison with <a href="#eq:dp-lower-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-lower-support">[eq:dp-lower-support]</a>, first for $h=\rho e$ and then for $h=-\rho e$, and passage to $\rho\downarrow0$, shows that $q=(y-x)/\ell$. The two quadratic remainders now prove differentiability and <a href="#eq:dp-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-gradient">[eq:dp-gradient]</a>–<a href="#eq:dp-supports" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-supports">[eq:dp-supports]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:dp-step-choice"></a>
<a id="eq:dp-velocities"></a>
Fix a state $z=(x,t)$ with $t>\epsilon$. Choose a maximizer for $\mathcal{P}_{2\epsilon}f(z)$ and a minimizer for $\mathcal{M}_{2\epsilon}v(z)$, and take $x^+$ and $x^-$ to be the respective intermediate points at time $t+\epsilon$ and $t-\epsilon$ on the straight paths to those optimizers. The composition formulas for the envelopes and additivity of the quadratic cost along a straight path give the following identities. Each half of a straight path has the same velocity and cost $\epsilon|Q_\pm|^2/2$. $$\label{eq:dp-step-choice}
\begin{aligned}
  f^{2\epsilon}(z)&=f^\epsilon(z^+)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}|Q_+|^2,
       &z^+&=(x^+,t+\epsilon),\\
  \mathcal{M}_{2\epsilon}v(z)&=g_\epsilon(z^-)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}|Q_-|^2,
       &z^-&=(x^-,t-\epsilon),
\end{aligned}$$ where $$\label{eq:dp-velocities}
  Q_+=\frac{x^+-x}{\epsilon}=\nabla_x f^{2\epsilon}(z),\qquad
  Q_-=\frac{x-x^-}{\epsilon},\qquad Q_0=\nabla_x f^\epsilon(z).$$ The formula for $Q_+$ follows from <a href="#eq:dp-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-gradient">[eq:dp-gradient]</a> for the full $2\epsilon$ optimizer. Although $z^-$ is the backward step, $Q_-$ is oriented forward from $x^-$ to $x$; the spatial increment for that step is $-\epsilon Q_-$. The backward optimizer need not be unique; any choice has the properties just stated.

<a id="stmt-lem-dp-drift"></a>
<div id="lem:dp-drift" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:dp-drift"></a>
**Lemma 24**. *At the state and steps above define $$\begin{aligned}
  D&=f(z)+f^{2\epsilon}(z)-2f^\epsilon(z),\\
  e&=f^\epsilon(z^-)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}|Q_-|^2-f(z),\\
  \widetilde D&=2g_\epsilon(z)-v(z)-\mathcal{M}_{2\epsilon}v(z),\\
  \widetilde e&=v(z)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}|Q_+|^2-g_\epsilon(z^+).
\end{aligned}$$ Here $D$ and $\widetilde D$ are the midpoint gaps for forward convexity and backward concavity. The quantities $e$ and $\widetilde e$ are the gaps from using $z$ as a competitor from the opposite step. All four quantities are nonnegative, and the exact drift identity and the velocity estimate are $$\label{eq:dp-drift}
\begin{split}
  \frac{J_\epsilon(z^+)+J_\epsilon(z^-)}{2}-J_\epsilon(z)
  &=\frac{D+e+\widetilde D+\widetilde e}{4\epsilon}\\
  &\ge\frac{D+e}{4\epsilon}\\
  &\ge c\bigl(|Q_+-Q_0|^2+|Q_--Q_0|^2\bigr),
  \qquad c=\frac{1}{16(1+\log2)}.
\end{split}$$*

</div>

For a fair choice between $z^+$ and $z^-$, the left side of <a href="#eq:dp-drift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-drift">[eq:dp-drift]</a> is the conditional expected change of $J_\epsilon$ given this state and the chosen optimizers. The drift therefore controls the squared discrepancies from the same reference velocity $Q_0$ at that state.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dp-drift"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dp-drift</summary>

*Proof.* Convexity in the length of $\mathcal{P}_\ell f(z)$ gives $D\ge0$, and concavity in the length of $\mathcal{M}_\ell v(z)$ gives $\widetilde D\ge0$. The point $z$ is a forward competitor from $z^-$ and a backward competitor from $z^+$, which gives $e\ge0$ and $\widetilde e\ge0$. The identities in <a href="#eq:dp-step-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-step-choice">[eq:dp-step-choice]</a> and the definitions of the competitor gaps yield $$\begin{aligned}
  f^\epsilon(z^+)+f^\epsilon(z^-)-2f^\epsilon(z)
    &=D+e+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\bigl(|Q_+|^2-|Q_-|^2\bigr),\\
  g_\epsilon(z^+)+g_\epsilon(z^-)-2g_\epsilon(z)
    &=-\widetilde D-\widetilde e
       +\frac{\epsilon}{2}\bigl(|Q_+|^2-|Q_-|^2\bigr).
\end{aligned}$$ The same difference of the two half-step costs occurs in both lines, so it cancels on subtraction. Dividing by $4\epsilon$ proves the identity and its first inequality.

<a id="eq:dp-path-midpoint"></a>
For the final inequality let $b,d\in\mathbb{R}^m$ be arbitrary. Apply the path-convexity property of the forward envelope to $$x(\ell)=x+d+\frac{\ell}{2\epsilon}(b-d),\qquad 0\le\ell\le2\epsilon.$$ For this path the function $\mathcal{P}_\ell f(x(\ell),t)+|b-d|^2\ell\log\ell/(4\epsilon^2)$ is convex, with $\ell\log\ell$ assigned its continuous value zero at $\ell=0$. The midpoint inequality, and $2\epsilon\log(2\epsilon)-2\epsilon\log\epsilon=2\epsilon\log2$, give $$\label{eq:dp-path-midpoint}
  f(x+d,t)+f^{2\epsilon}(x+b,t)
    -2f^\epsilon\left(x+\frac{b+d}{2},t\right)
  \ge-\frac{\log2}{2\epsilon}|b-d|^2.$$ Using $x+d$ as a forward competitor from $z^-$ and expanding its cost gives $$f(x+d,t)\le f(z)+e+Q_-\cdot d+\frac{|d|^2}{2\epsilon}.$$ Use the upper supporting inequality in <a href="#eq:dp-supports" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-supports">[eq:dp-supports]</a> for $f^{2\epsilon}(x+b,t)$, and the lower one for $f^\epsilon(x+(b+d)/2,t)$. The left side of <a href="#eq:dp-path-midpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-path-midpoint">[eq:dp-path-midpoint]</a> is then at most $$D+e+(Q_+-Q_0)\cdot b+(Q_--Q_0)\cdot d
       +\frac{|b|^2}{2t}+\frac{|d|^2}{2\epsilon}
       +\frac{|b+d|^2}{4\epsilon}.$$ Combining this upper bound with <a href="#eq:dp-path-midpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-path-midpoint">[eq:dp-path-midpoint]</a>, using $t>\epsilon$ and $|b\pm d|^2\le2(|b|^2+|d|^2)$, proves $$D+e\ge (Q_0-Q_+)\cdot b+(Q_0-Q_-)\cdot d
            -\frac{1+\log2}{\epsilon}(|b|^2+|d|^2).$$ Choose $$b=\frac{\epsilon(Q_0-Q_+)}{2(1+\log2)},\qquad
  d=\frac{\epsilon(Q_0-Q_-)}{2(1+\log2)}.$$ The result is $D+e\ge\epsilon(|Q_+-Q_0|^2+|Q_--Q_0|^2)/(4(1+\log2))$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Fitted frames and a logarithmic velocity bound

The walks will later be viewed in frames based at fits at smaller positive times. We first show that the squared discrepancies in <a href="#eq:dp-drift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-drift">[eq:dp-drift]</a> are unchanged in those frames, and that the transformed solution remains in the standing family. The velocity estimate following this calculation will then apply with constants independent of the frame.

<a id="stmt-lem-fitted-frame-covariance"></a>
<div id="lem:fitted-frame-covariance" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:contact-frame"></a>
<a id="eq:contact-W-covariance"></a>
<a id="eq:fitted-slope-covariance"></a>
<a id="eq:contact-velocity-covariance"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Covariance in a fitted frame). *Let $r>0$, and let $(p_0,a_0)$ be a fit for $v$ at $(x_0,r)$ of radius $r$. At corresponding points define $$\label{eq:contact-frame}
\begin{gathered}
  s=\frac{t}{r},\qquad y=\frac{x-x_0-p_0(t-r)}{r},\\
  x=x_0+r\bigl(y+p_0(s-1)\bigr),\qquad t=rs,\\
  \bar v(y,s)=\frac{v(x,t)-v(x_0,r)}{r}
          -p_0\cdot y-\left(a_0+\frac{|p_0|^2}{2}\right)(s-1).
\end{gathered}$$ Then $\bar v\in\mathcal{A}_{m,C_0}$ is centered at $(0,1)$ and satisfies <a href="#eq:jensen-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jensen-bound">[eq:jensen-bound]</a> with $f=\bar v$, $I=\mathbb{R}$, and the same $\kappa$. Define $$\bar W(y,s)=\mathcal{C}_s\bar v(y,s)-\frac{\kappa^2s}{2}\qquad(s>0).$$ For every $\sigma>0$, the barycentric and $W$ gaps satisfy $$\label{eq:contact-W-covariance}
\begin{split}
  \mathcal{C}_\sigma\bar v(y,s)-\bar v(y,s)
       &=\frac{\mathcal{C}_{r\sigma}v(x,t)-v(x,t)}{r},\\
  \bar W(y,s)-\bar v(y,s)&=\frac{W(x,t)-v(x,t)}{r}
       \qquad(s>0).
\end{split}$$ For every $\ell>0$, the solution slopes satisfy $$\label{eq:fitted-slope-covariance}
  \mathcal{S}_\ell^\pm\bar v(y,s)=\mathcal{S}_{r\ell}^\pm v(x,t)-a_0.$$ If the steps in <a href="#eq:dp-step-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-step-choice">[eq:dp-step-choice]</a> are chosen at any state with $t>\epsilon$, their images are optimizing steps for the transformed pair, with step size $\epsilon'=\epsilon/r$. Their velocities satisfy $$\label{eq:contact-velocity-covariance}
\begin{gathered}
  Q'_+=\frac{y^+-y}{\epsilon'}=Q_+-p_0,\qquad
  Q'_- =\frac{y-y^-}{\epsilon'}=Q_--p_0,\\
  Q'_0=\nabla_y\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon'}\bar W(y,s)=Q_0-p_0,\qquad
  Q'_\pm-Q'_0=Q_\pm-Q_0.
\end{gathered}$$ All these identities are global at corresponding states, with the indicated positive-time restriction for $W$; the states need not lie in the fitting box. No contact at $(x_0,r)$ is assumed. The centering and fit assertion concerns $\bar v$ alone.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fitted-frame-covariance"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fitted-frame-covariance</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:frame-invariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frame-invariance">14</a>, followed by the time translation that puts the fitted point at $(0,1)$, proves the class and centering assertions. At each fixed time the spatial offsets are multiplied by $r$, and values are multiplied by $r$ up to an affine function. The affine terms cancel from a barycentric defect, so its quotient by the root mean square radius is unchanged. This proves the Jensen assertion.

For the remaining calculations put $$A(y,s)=\frac{v(x_0,r)}{r}+p_0\cdot y
             +\left(a_0+\frac{|p_0|^2}{2}\right)(s-1),
  \qquad \frac{v(x,t)}{r}=\bar v(y,s)+A(y,s).$$ Scaling the positions of a barycentric configuration and cancelling its weighted affine terms gives $$\frac{\mathcal{C}_{r\sigma}v(x,t)}{r}
       =\mathcal{C}_\sigma\bar v(y,s)+A(y,s).$$ Subtracting the displayed formula for $v/r$ proves the first identity in <a href="#eq:contact-W-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-W-covariance">[eq:contact-W-covariance]</a>. For $s>0$, take $\sigma=s$. The old time and the old barycentric parameter in $W$ are both $t=rs$, while the correction $\kappa^2t/(2r)$ is $\kappa^2s/2$. Hence $$\frac{W(x,t)}{r}=\bar W(y,s)+A(y,s),$$ which proves the second gap identity. This is why the frame uses $s=t/r$: it preserves the time-zero boundary in the definition of $W$.

<a id="eq:envelope-covariance"></a>
The quadratic envelopes obey the following value identities for $\ell>0$, with $s>0$ in the identity involving $W$: $$\label{eq:envelope-covariance}
\begin{split}
  \frac{\mathcal{P}_{r\ell}v(x,t)}{r}
    &=\mathcal{P}_\ell\bar v(y,s)+A(y,s)+a_0\ell,\\
  \frac{\mathcal{M}_{r\ell}v(x,t)}{r}
    &=\mathcal{M}_\ell\bar v(y,s)+A(y,s)-a_0\ell,\\
  \frac{\mathcal{P}_{r\ell}W(x,t)}{r}
    &=\mathcal{P}_\ell\bar W(y,s)+A(y,s)+a_0\ell.
\end{split}$$ To check the first identity, parameterize an old forward competitor by a new position $y'$ at time $s+\ell$. Its old displacement divided by $r$ is $y'-y+p_0\ell$. The cost divided by $r$ is $$\frac{|y'-y+p_0\ell|^2}{2\ell}
    =\frac{|y'-y|^2}{2\ell}+p_0\cdot(y'-y)
                                     +\frac{|p_0|^2\ell}{2}.$$ The affine change in $A$ from $(y,s)$ to $(y',s+\ell)$ is $p_0\cdot(y'-y)+(a_0+|p_0|^2/2)\ell$, leaving $a_0\ell$ after the cost is subtracted. For a backward competitor the old displacement divided by $r$ is $y'-y-p_0\ell$; the same expansion with the cost added leaves $-a_0\ell$. These prove the first two identities. The last follows by the same forward calculation using $W/r=\bar W+A$ at every positive time. Subtract the formula for $v/r$ from the forward identity, and subtract the backward identity from that formula. Dividing each result by $\ell$ gives <a href="#eq:fitted-slope-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fitted-slope-covariance">[eq:fitted-slope-covariance]</a>.

The competitor calculations also show that old optimizers map to optimizers for the transformed envelopes. The affine coordinate map sends their straight intermediate points to the new straight intermediate points. Thus the mapped steps are precisely the steps of <a href="#eq:dp-step-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-step-choice">[eq:dp-step-choice]</a> for the transformed pair. The first two velocity equalities in <a href="#eq:contact-velocity-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-velocity-covariance">[eq:contact-velocity-covariance]</a> follow from <a href="#eq:contact-frame" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-frame">[eq:contact-frame]</a> and $t^\pm-t=\pm\epsilon$. For the reference velocity, differentiate the last identity of <a href="#eq:envelope-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:envelope-covariance">[eq:envelope-covariance]</a> in $y$ with $r\ell=\epsilon$. The derivative of the left side is $\nabla_x\mathcal{P}_\epsilon W=Q_0$, since $D_yx=rI$, and the derivative of $A$ is $p_0$. This proves the equality for $Q'_0$ and the invariance of the velocity discrepancies, with no additional scale factor. ◻

</details>

It remains to bound the reference velocity in each of these centered frames. As before, write $L(K)=1+\log K$ for $K\ge2$. The next bound is uniform over the standing family. Its squared logarithmic singularity in time is integrable near zero, which will control the expected martingale variation through the time-walk occupation estimates.

<a id="stmt-lem-dp-log-velocity"></a>
<div id="lem:dp-log-velocity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26**. *Suppose $K\ge2$, $|x|,|t|\le K$, $0<\epsilon\le1$, and $t>\epsilon$. Then the velocity in <a href="#eq:dp-velocities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-velocities">[eq:dp-velocities]</a> satisfies <a id="eq:dp-log-velocity"></a>$$\label{eq:dp-log-velocity}
  |Q_0|=|\nabla_x\mathcal{P}_\epsilon W(x,t)|
       \le C\bigl(L(K)+|\log t|\bigr).$$ Here $C$ depends only on the fitting constant, the spatial dimension, and $\kappa$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dp-log-velocity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dp-log-velocity</summary>

<a id="eq:dp-W-upper-support"></a>
<a id="eq:dp-support-slope-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Let $s=t+\epsilon$, so $2\epsilon<s\le K+1$. At the point $x$, choose any upper supporting slope $p$ for the semiconcave function $f(\cdot,s)$: $$\label{eq:dp-W-upper-support}
  f(x+h,s)\le f(x,s)+p\cdot h+\frac{|h|^2}{2s}
  \quad(h\in\mathbb{R}^m).$$ Such a slope exists because $f(\cdot,s)-|\cdot|^2/(2s)$ is finite and concave on all of $\mathbb{R}^m$. If $p\ne0$, insert $h=-sp/|p|$ to obtain $$f(x+h,s)-f(x,s)\le-s|p|+\frac{s}{2}.$$ The sandwich bound for $W$ gives, in the other direction, $$f(x+h,s)-f(x,s)
    \ge v(x+h,s)-v(x,s)-\frac{\kappa^2s}{2}.$$ The spatial increment estimate from the fits, applied with increment length $s$ at the center $(x,s)$ in the box of size $2K$, bounds the right side below by $$-Cs\bigl(L(2K)+|\log s|\bigr)-\frac{\kappa^2s}{2}.$$ The resulting estimate for $p$ also holds when $p=0$. Since $1<s/t<2$, we have $|\log s|\le|\log t|+\log2$, and hence $$\label{eq:dp-support-slope-bound}
  |p|\le C\bigl(L(K)+|\log t|\bigr).$$

Let $h$ be the optimizing displacement for $f^\epsilon(x,t)$. Its value is at least the value of the competitor $h=0$. In conjunction with <a href="#eq:dp-W-upper-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-W-upper-support">[eq:dp-W-upper-support]</a>, this gives $$\frac{|h|^2}{2\epsilon}\le p\cdot h+\frac{|h|^2}{2s}.$$ For $h\ne0$, using $s>2\epsilon$ yields $|h|/\epsilon\le4|p|$; the same inequality is immediate when $h=0$. By <a href="#eq:dp-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-gradient">[eq:dp-gradient]</a>, $Q_0=h/\epsilon$, so <a href="#eq:dp-support-slope-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-support-slope-bound">[eq:dp-support-slope-bound]</a> proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:contact-localization"></a>
### Propagation to time zero and localization

We now apply the discrete inequalities to the extremal pair $v,W$ and the constant $\kappa$ supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:envelope-contact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:envelope-contact">22</a>. Write $z_*=(0,1)$ and $H_*=H_v(z_*)\in\mathbb{R}$; the starting limit is <a href="#eq:dp-J-contact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-J-contact">[eq:dp-J-contact]</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-contact-propagation"></a>
<div id="prop:contact-propagation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:contact-propagation-conclusion"></a>
**Proposition 27**. *Put $\delta_j=2^{-j}$ for $j\ge1$. There exist a constant $K<\infty$, a point $x_{\rm end}\in\mathbb{R}^m$, and an infinite set $\mathcal J\subset\mathbb N$ with the following property. For every $j\in\mathcal J$ there are a point $x_j\in\mathbb{R}^m$ and a fit $(p_j,a_j)$ for $v$ at $(x_j,\delta_j)$ of radius $\delta_j$, in the sense of <a href="#eq:limit-fit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-fit">[eq:limit-fit]</a>, such that $$\label{eq:contact-propagation-conclusion}
\begin{gathered}
  H_v(x_{\rm end},0)>-\infty,\qquad
  W(x_j,\delta_j)=v(x_j,\delta_j),\\
  |x_{\rm end}-x_j+p_j\delta_j|\le K\delta_j.
\end{gathered}$$*

</div>

The last bound says that the same terminal point stays in a fixed bounded part of every selected fitted frame, where its finite slope will identify the limiting fixed-length slopes. Figure <a href="#fig:contact-localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:contact-localization">2</a> shows how two selected fitted frames localize the same endpoint.

<figure id="fig:contact-localization">

<figcaption>Schematic localization at two selected scales, shown in one spatial direction with different horizontal and vertical units. The dashed axes belong to the two fitted frames; each shaded endpoint-localization strip has spatial half-width $Kδ_{j}$ or $Kδ_{k}$. Their time-zero sections both contain the same point $(x_{\rm end},0)$, expressing $|x_{\rm end}-x_j+p_j\delta_j|\le K\delta_j$. The conclusion holds for infinitely many selected indices $j ∈ 𝒥$. The axes are separate affine fits, not a trajectory joining the marks.</figcaption>
</figure>
