<details id="proof-stmt-prop-contact-propagation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-contact-propagation</summary>

*Proof.* We first construct finite stopped walks with common expectation and error bounds, and then localize their terminal points in the frames of their marks. Finally, one joint limiting law will retain the terminal point and all marks, allowing us to select infinitely many localized contacts for the same endpoint.

*The stopped walks and their expectation bounds.* Fix for the moment an integer $T\ge2$ and a radius $R\ge2$. For each $N\ge1$ choose $\epsilon_N$ to be an inverse power of two with <a id="eq:contact-grid"></a>$$\label{eq:contact-grid}
  0<\epsilon_N\le\frac{\delta_N}{4}.$$ In particular, $\epsilon_N$ divides $1,T$, and every $\delta_j$ with $j\le N$. We will make each $\epsilon_N$ smaller if necessary, retaining these conditions. Write $\epsilon=\epsilon_N$ while constructing the $N$-th walk.

Start at $z_0=z_*$. At a state $z_n=(x_n,t_n)$ with $\delta_N<t_n<T$ and $|x_n|<R$, choose the two steps of <a href="#eq:dp-step-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-step-choice">[eq:dp-step-choice]</a> and take $z_{n+1}=z_n^+$ or $z_n^-$ with equal probabilities. Stop on first reaching $t_n=\delta_N$, $t_n=T$, or $|x_n|\ge R$; call this stopping index $\tau_N$. The choices of optimizers can be made separately at every node of the countable tree of finite histories. Taking independent fair signs $\xi_n\in\{-1,1\}$ to select the steps then defines the walk and the filtration $\mathcal F_n=\sigma(\xi_0,\ldots,\xi_{n-1})$; all velocities at step $n$ are $\mathcal F_n$-measurable. In expressions such as $Q_-(z_n)$ below, the velocity denotes the choice made at that history. Before stopping, $t_n>\delta_N\ge4\epsilon$, so the inequality <a href="#eq:dp-drift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-drift">[eq:dp-drift]</a> applies.

<a id="eq:contact-start-lower"></a>
We choose $\epsilon_N$ sufficiently small that every position up to and including the stop lies in $\overline B_{R+1}(0)$. Here is the uniform localization of optimizers that permits this choice. The sandwich for $W$ extends it continuously to $t=0$ by $W(x,0)=v(x,0)$. The growth bounds for $v$ and the sandwich give uniform subquadratic growth for $v$ and $W$ on compact time intervals in $\mathbb{R}$ and $[0,\infty)$, respectively. For base points in a fixed compact box and lengths $\ell\le1$, a quadratic cost first confines all optimizing positions for $\mathcal{P}_\ell W$ and $\mathcal{M}_\ell v$ to a fixed spatial compact set: outside a sufficiently large ball the cost dominates the uniform subquadratic bound, even with the weaker coefficient corresponding to $\ell=1$. On that compact set the functions are bounded. Comparing an optimizer with the zero displacement competitor then gives $|y-x|^2/(2\ell)\le C$ there, uniformly as $\ell\downarrow0$. Thus the optimizing displacements tend to zero uniformly. Apply this to length $2\epsilon$ and base points with $|x|\le R$, $0\le t\le T$, in a slightly larger time interval. The intermediate displacements used for the steps are half the full displacements, so they can all be made at most one. This proves the asserted bound at stopping. We also choose $\epsilon_N$ small enough that $$\label{eq:contact-start-lower}
  J_{\epsilon_N}(z_*)\ge H_*-1,$$ which is possible by <a href="#eq:dp-J-contact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-J-contact">[eq:dp-J-contact]</a>.

<a id="eq:contact-J-upper"></a>
The time coordinate before stopping is the simple symmetric walk with step $\epsilon$ starting at $1$. It is stopped no later than this time walk hits $\delta_N$ or $T$. The latter hitting time is almost surely finite and has finite expectation, for example by the elementary gambler’s ruin formula. The same is therefore true of $\tau_N$. Put $$\mathcal B=\overline B_{R+1}(0)\times[0,T],\qquad B=R+T+2.$$ The upper slope bound for $v$ on boxes and <a href="#eq:dp-J-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-J-bounds">[eq:dp-J-bounds]</a> give a constant $U_B=C L(B)^4$, independent of $N$, such that $$\label{eq:contact-J-upper}
  J_{\epsilon_N}(z_n)\le U_B\qquad (0\le n\le\tau_N).$$

<a id="eq:contact-truncated-drift"></a>
<a id="eq:contact-stopped-expectation"></a>
<a id="eq:contact-error-budget"></a>
For any stopping time $\sigma\le\tau_N$, summing the conditional form of <a href="#eq:dp-drift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-drift">[eq:dp-drift]</a> up to $\sigma\wedge h$ gives $$\label{eq:contact-truncated-drift}
\begin{split}
  \mathbb E J_\epsilon(z_{\sigma\wedge h})
  &\ge J_\epsilon(z_*)\\
  &\quad+c\,\mathbb E\sum_{n<\sigma\wedge h}
          \bigl(|Q_+(z_n)-Q_0(z_n)|^2+|Q_-(z_n)-Q_0(z_n)|^2\bigr).
\end{split}$$ For each fixed $N$, $J_\epsilon$ is continuous and bounded on the compact box $\overline B_{R+1}(0)\times[\delta_N,T]$ containing the stopped walk. Thus dominated convergence applies to its term as $h\to\infty$. Monotone convergence applies to the nonnegative sum. We obtain, for all these stopping times, $$\label{eq:contact-stopped-expectation}
  \mathbb E J_\epsilon(z_\sigma)\ge J_\epsilon(z_*).$$ Taking $\sigma=\tau_N$ in <a href="#eq:contact-truncated-drift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-truncated-drift">[eq:contact-truncated-drift]</a> and using <a href="#eq:contact-start-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-start-lower">[eq:contact-start-lower]</a>–<a href="#eq:contact-J-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-J-upper">[eq:contact-J-upper]</a> also gives $$\label{eq:contact-error-budget}
  \mathbb E\sum_{n<\tau_N}
       \bigl(|Q_+(z_n)-Q_0(z_n)|^2+|Q_-(z_n)-Q_0(z_n)|^2\bigr)
  \le B_0,\qquad B_0=C_*L(B)^4.$$ Here $C_*$ is fixed for $v$ and independent of $N,R,T$; it absorbs the finite number $H_*$ and the absolute constant $c$ in <a href="#eq:dp-drift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-drift">[eq:dp-drift]</a>. Both <a href="#eq:contact-stopped-expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-stopped-expectation">[eq:contact-stopped-expectation]</a> and <a href="#eq:contact-error-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-error-budget">[eq:contact-error-budget]</a> are unconditional expectations. Together with the upper slope bound, the lower expectation bound will give contact and a finite terminal slope in the limit. We first use the discrepancy bound to control horizontal-exit probabilities, both in the original coordinates and in fitted frames.

*Reaching the lower level with positive probability.* We next show how to choose the outer cutoffs so that the walks reach $t=\delta_N$ with probability at least $3/4$, uniformly in $N$. Couple the time signs with the simple symmetric walk starting at $1$ and stopped only at $0$ or $T$. Its probability of reaching $T$ is $1/T$, which also bounds that probability for our walk, since it may stop sooner. To control horizontal exit, we will bound the expected maximal displacement by $C_T L(R+T+2)^2$, with $C_T$ independent of $R$ and $N$. Markov’s inequality will then make the probability of horizontal exit tend to zero as $R\to\infty$ for fixed $T$.

<a id="eq:contact-time-and-velocity-budget"></a>
The expected number of visits of the unrestricted time walk to any fixed interior grid level is at most $CT/\epsilon$. To verify this directly, write $q=T/\epsilon$ and identify the level with $k\in\{1,\ldots,q-1\}$. After a visit to $k$, the probability of reaching an endpoint before the next return is $$\frac12\left(\frac1k+\frac1{q-k}\right)\ge\frac2q.$$ This follows from the linear hitting probability for a symmetric walk on each of the intervals $[0,k]$ and $[k,q]$. Starting from the first visit, the expected total number of visits is at most $q/2$, by the geometric return calculation; the probability of that first visit is at most one. Stopping the actual walk earlier can only decrease its visit counts. Summing this bound over levels gives $$\label{eq:contact-time-and-velocity-budget}
  \mathbb E(\epsilon^2\tau_N)\le CT^2,\qquad
  \mathbb E\sum_{n<\tau_N}\epsilon^2|Q_0(z_n)|^2
       \le C_T L(B)^2.$$ For the second inequality we used Lemma <a href="#lem:dp-log-velocity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dp-log-velocity">26</a> before each step. More explicitly, it bounds the summand velocity squared by $C(L(B)^2+|\log t_n|^2)$, while the visit estimate gives at most $$CT\epsilon\sum_{k=1}^{q-1}
           \bigl(L(B)^2+|\log(k\epsilon)|^2\bigr).$$ This has the asserted bound. Indeed, on $(0,1]$ the function $|\log s|^2$ is decreasing and integrable, so its right-endpoint grid sum times $\epsilon$ is at most its integral there; on $[1,T]$ it is bounded by $(\log T)^2$. The constants $C_T$ in this proof can depend on $T$ and the fixed data, but not on $R$ or $N$.

<a id="eq:contact-travel-inequality"></a>
<a id="eq:contact-original-travel"></a>
For $n<\tau_N$ the spatial increment can be written exactly as $$x_{n+1}-x_n
   =\epsilon\xi_n Q_0(z_n)
     +\epsilon\xi_n\bigl(Q_{\xi_n}(z_n)-Q_0(z_n)\bigr),$$ where $Q_{1}=Q_+$ and $Q_{-1}=Q_-$. Extend both kinds of increments by zero after stopping. The first summands are martingale differences, since the active-step indicator and $Q_0(z_n)$ are known before the fair sign. For a deterministic truncation in the number of steps, Doob’s $L^2$ maximal inequality and orthogonality of these differences give an upper bound $$2\left(\mathbb E\sum_{n<\tau_N}\epsilon^2|Q_0(z_n)|^2\right)^{1/2}$$ for the expected supremum of the norm of their partial sums. Letting the truncation increase preserves this bound by monotone convergence of the suprema. On each path, the sum of the norms of the other increments is at most $$(\epsilon^2\tau_N)^{1/2}
  \left(\sum_{n<\tau_N}
       \bigl(|Q_+(z_n)-Q_0(z_n)|^2+|Q_-(z_n)-Q_0(z_n)|^2\bigr)\right)^{1/2}.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz in expectation gives the travel inequality $$\label{eq:contact-travel-inequality}
\begin{split}
  \mathbb E\sup_{0\le n\le\tau_N}|x_n|
  &\le 2\left(\mathbb E\sum_{n<\tau_N}
                    \epsilon^2|Q_0(z_n)|^2\right)^{1/2}\\
  &\quad+\left(\mathbb E(\epsilon^2\tau_N)\right)^{1/2}\\
  &\qquad\times\left(\mathbb E\sum_{n<\tau_N}
       \bigl(|Q_+(z_n)-Q_0(z_n)|^2+|Q_-(z_n)-Q_0(z_n)|^2\bigr)\right)^{1/2}.
\end{split}$$ This calculation uses only predictability of the coefficients of the fair signs in the reference increments, including the active-step indicators, and the pathwise bound on error lengths. It therefore applies as well to the displacement from the starting point of a stopped portion after a stopping time, with its increments extended by zero outside that portion. Equations <a href="#eq:contact-error-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-error-budget">[eq:contact-error-budget]</a> and <a href="#eq:contact-time-and-velocity-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-time-and-velocity-budget">[eq:contact-time-and-velocity-budget]</a> now show that $$\label{eq:contact-original-travel}
  \mathbb E\sup_{0\le n\le\tau_N}|x_n|
    \le C_T\bigl(L(B)+\sqrt{B_0}\bigr)
    \le C'_T L(B)^2.$$

<a id="eq:contact-lower-hit-probability"></a>
The probability of a horizontal stop is at most the right side of <a href="#eq:contact-original-travel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-original-travel">[eq:contact-original-travel]</a> divided by $R$. For fixed $T$ this tends to zero as $R\to\infty$. Choose an integer $T\ge8$, and then choose $R$ large enough that the horizontal-stop probability is at most $1/8$, uniformly in $N$. Fix these $T,R$ for the rest of the proof, so $B$ and $B_0$ are now fixed finite constants. If $$\eta_N=\mathbf1_{\{t_{\tau_N}=\delta_N\}},$$ the two preceding bounds and the definition of the stop imply $$\label{eq:contact-lower-hit-probability}
  \mathbb P(\eta_N=1)\ge\frac34\qquad(N\ge1).$$ This remains true if the lower time level and the horizontal cutoff are reached simultaneously, since that case has $\eta_N=1$.

*Marks and localization in fitted frames.* For $1\le j\le N$, let $$\sigma_{j,N}=\tau_N\wedge\inf\{n\ge0:t_n=\delta_j\},\qquad
  z_j=(x_j,t_j)=z_{\sigma_{j,N}}.$$ Thus a mark is the first point on its time level, or the terminal point if the walk stops before that level. The divisibility of the grid ensures $t_j\ge\delta_j$, with equality on $\{\eta_N=1\}$. At the time of this mark choose a fit $(p_j,a_j)$ at $z_j$ of radius $\delta_j$. These choices can again be made on the countable tree of histories, so the parameters are $\mathcal F_{\sigma_{j,N}}$-measurable. The bound on fit parameters on boxes gives, for a fixed constant independent of $N$, $$|p_j|+|a_j|\le\Gamma_j:=C\bigl(L(B)+j\bigr),\qquad j\le N,$$ increasing $C$ to make every $\Gamma_j\ge1$.

The cutoffs $T,R$ and the error bound $B_0$ are now fixed. We will choose two further cutoffs, uniformly in $j,N$, to control the terminal point in each fitted frame. Fix $1\le j\le N$, put $r=\delta_j$, and let $\mathcal R_{j,N}=\{t_j=r\}$ be the event that the level $r$ is reached, including the case where it is reached at the original stop. This event is $\mathcal F_{\sigma_{j,N}}$-measurable. On it $z_j=(x_j,r)$, so we may apply Lemma <a href="#lem:fitted-frame-covariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fitted-frame-covariance">25</a> pathwise with the chosen fit $(p_j,a_j)$. In its coordinates, $$s=\frac{t}{r},\qquad
  y=\frac{x-x_j-p_j(t-r)}{r},\qquad
  \epsilon'=\frac{\epsilon_N}{r}.$$ The transformed solution $\bar v$ is centered at $(0,1)$ and has the same Jensen bound. Its $\bar W$ therefore has the standing properties of Section <a href="#sec:discrete-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:discrete-propagation">6</a>. The mapped steps are optimizing steps, and <a href="#eq:contact-velocity-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-velocity-covariance">[eq:contact-velocity-covariance]</a> gives identical squared velocity discrepancies. Contact at this finite mark is not needed. The fit and frame are known at the marking time and will be held fixed afterward.

On $\mathcal R_{j,N}$ consider the portion of the original walk from its $j$-th mark to its stop, with the additional rule that this portion stops on first reaching $s=M$ or $|y|\ge K$, where $M\ge2$ is an integer and $K\ge2$. It includes the endpoint of this additional stopping rule. If the original walk stopped at the mark, the portion has no steps. A hit of an additional cutoff simultaneous with the original stop is counted as a hit of that additional cutoff. On $\mathcal R_{j,N}^{\rm c}$ define the portion to be empty as well, and assign zero to all its sums and spatial suprema. Let $n'$ be its number of steps, and write $\sum'$ for a sum over its pre-step states.

<a id="eq:contact-frame-error-budget"></a>
The total squared velocity error on this portion, in the new frame, has the unconditional bound $$\label{eq:contact-frame-error-budget}
  \mathbb E\sum'
       \bigl(|Q'_+-Q'_0|^2+|Q'_--Q'_0|^2\bigr)\le B_0.$$ Indeed, on each path the sum is a subsum of the original sum in <a href="#eq:contact-error-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-error-budget">[eq:contact-error-budget]</a>, with identical summands by <a href="#eq:contact-velocity-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-velocity-covariance">[eq:contact-velocity-covariance]</a>; on an empty portion it is zero. This argument gives an unconditional estimate. It makes no assertion that the same bound holds after conditioning on a particular history at the mark.

<a id="eq:contact-frame-time-budget"></a>
The time-walk estimates, in contrast, do hold conditionally on that history. On $\mathcal R_{j,N}$ the new time starts at $s=1$ and has step $\epsilon'=\epsilon_N/r\le1/4$, which divides $1$ and $M$. Given $\mathcal F_{\sigma_{j,N}}$, the subsequent signs remain fair. The portion is stopped no later than the unrestricted new time walk first hits $0$ or $M$. Its conditional expected visits to each interior level are therefore at most $CM/\epsilon'$ by the same visit argument used above. Before any step of the portion, the original walk is still live, so $s=t/r>\delta_N/r\ge4\epsilon'$. Also $|y|<K$ and $s<M$ before the additional stop. Lemma <a href="#lem:dp-log-velocity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dp-log-velocity">26</a> applies to the centered $\bar v$ and its $\bar W$ uniformly, and bounds $|Q'_0|$ by $C(L(K+M+2)+|\log s|)$. The integrable logarithmic grid sum gives $$\label{eq:contact-frame-time-budget}
\begin{split}
  \mathbb E\bigl((\epsilon')^2n'\mid\mathcal F_{\sigma_{j,N}}\bigr)
       &\le CM^2\mathbf1_{\mathcal R_{j,N}},\\
  \mathbb E\left(\sum'
          (\epsilon')^2|Q'_0|^2\,\middle|\,\mathcal F_{\sigma_{j,N}}\right)
       &\le C_M L(K+M+2)^2\mathbf1_{\mathcal R_{j,N}}.
\end{split}$$ The frame is known at the conditioning time, and the estimates of the centered class have constants independent of that frame. This explains the uniformity of these conditional bounds. Integrating them gives the same bounds unconditionally without the indicator.

<a id="eq:contact-frame-travel"></a>
Extend the new increments by zero outside the portion. Their reference part is $\epsilon'\xi_nQ'_0$ and their error part is $\epsilon'\xi_n(Q'_{\xi_n}-Q'_0)$. The reference increments are martingale differences after the random starting mark: the fit and reached event are known at that mark, and on an active step the indicator and $Q'_0=Q_0-p_j$ are $\mathcal F_n$-measurable while $\xi_n$ is still fair given $\mathcal F_n$. Thus the calculation of <a href="#eq:contact-travel-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-travel-inequality">[eq:contact-travel-inequality]</a> applies in the primed variables. Using the integrated bounds from <a href="#eq:contact-frame-time-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-frame-time-budget">[eq:contact-frame-time-budget]</a> and the unconditional bound <a href="#eq:contact-frame-error-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-frame-error-budget">[eq:contact-frame-error-budget]</a>, it gives $$\label{eq:contact-frame-travel}
  \mathbb E\left(\sup_{\text{points of the portion}}|y|\right)
   \le C'_M\bigl(L(K+M+2)+\sqrt{B_0}\bigr).$$ The supremum is understood to be zero for an empty portion. In particular, the probability that this portion reaches $|y|\ge K$ is at most the right side of <a href="#eq:contact-frame-travel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-frame-travel">[eq:contact-frame-travel]</a> divided by $K$. The probability it reaches $s=M$ is at most $1/M$: conditionally on reaching the mark, the unrestricted symmetric time walk starting from $1$ hits $M$ before $0$ with probability $1/M$, and an earlier stop only removes this event.

<a id="eq:contact-finite-localization"></a>
Choose a fixed integer $M\ge8$, and then choose $K$ so large that $$\frac{C'_M\bigl(L(K+M+2)+\sqrt{B_0}\bigr)}{K}\le\frac18.$$ This is possible because $B_0$ is already fixed and $L(K+M+2)/K\to0$. On $\{\eta_N=1\}$ the $j$-th level is reached. If neither additional cutoff intervenes, the terminal point is in the portion and has $|y|\le K$. Combining this observation with <a href="#eq:contact-lower-hit-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-lower-hit-probability">[eq:contact-lower-hit-probability]</a> and the two additional-cutoff probabilities proves, uniformly for $j\le N$, $$\label{eq:contact-finite-localization}
  \mathbb P\left(\eta_N=1,\quad
      |x_{\tau_N}-x_j-p_j(t_{\tau_N}-\delta_j)|\le K\delta_j\right)
       \ge\frac34-\frac18-\frac18=\frac12.$$

*One limiting law and the contact properties.* We use one fixed compact space for all these laws. Define $$\mathcal K_j=\mathcal B\times\overline B_{\Gamma_j}(0)
                    \times[-\Gamma_j,\Gamma_j],
  \qquad
  \mathcal X=\mathcal B\times\{0,1\}\times\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathcal K_j.$$ The coordinates are the terminal point, the flag, and the marked point and fit at each index. This countable product of compact metric spaces is compact and metrizable. For indices $j>N$, fill the coordinates by any fixed point of $\mathcal K_j$; these dummy entries need not be fits. Let $\mu_N$ be the law on $\mathcal X$ of the terminal point $z_{\tau_N}$, the flag $\eta_N$, and the marked points and fits. Compactness of the space of probability measures on a compact metric space gives a subsequence converging weakly to a probability law $\mu$; all subsequent limits in $N$ are along this subsequence. Denote its coordinates by $z_{\rm end}=(x_{\rm end},t_{\rm end})$, $\eta$, and $(z_j,p_j,a_j)$.

<a id="eq:contact-mark-limit"></a>
Here are first the properties that pass directly to this law. The flag is a coordinate in a discrete space, so its probability converges and $\mu(\eta=1)\ge3/4$. Also the continuous nonnegative function $\eta t_{\rm end}$ on $\mathcal X$ has integral at most $\delta_N$ under $\mu_N$. Its integral under $\mu$ is zero. It follows that $t_{\rm end}=0$ almost surely on $\{\eta=1\}$. For each fixed $j$, the sets $\{t_j\ge\delta_j\}$ and $\{\eta=0\}\cup\{t_j=\delta_j\}$ are closed and have $\mu_N$-measure one for all $N\ge j$. The closed-set direction of the Portmanteau Theorem, $\limsup_N\mu_N(F)\le\mu(F)$, makes their $\mu$-measures one. Fits at the specified radius form a closed subset of $\mathcal K_j$. To see this without varying the sheared domain, write its increments in <a href="#eq:limit-fit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-fit">[eq:limit-fit]</a> as $$\ell=\delta_j\theta,\qquad
  h=p_j\delta_j\theta+\delta_j\zeta,
  \qquad |\theta|\le1,\quad |\zeta|\le1.$$ For each $(\theta,\zeta)$ the fitting inequality is closed in $(z_j,p_j,a_j)$ by continuity of $v$, and their intersection over this fixed compact set of increments is closed. Portmanteau therefore also passes the fitting property to $\mu$. Taking the intersection of the resulting full-measure sets for all $j$, we have $$\label{eq:contact-mark-limit}
\begin{gathered}
  t_{\rm end}=0\text{ on }\{\eta=1\},\qquad
  t_j\ge\delta_j\text{ and }t_j=\delta_j\text{ on }\{\eta=1\},\\
  (p_j,a_j)\text{ is a fit at }z_j\text{ of radius }\delta_j
       \quad\text{for every }j,
\end{gathered}$$ almost surely.

<a id="eq:contact-limit-properties"></a>
<a id="eq:contact-terminal-slopes"></a>
Two further properties come from the expectation bounds: $$\label{eq:contact-limit-properties}
  H_v(z_{\rm end})>-\infty,\qquad W(z_j)=v(z_j)\quad(j\ge1)
  \qquad\text{almost surely under }\mu.$$ For the first, fix $\ell>0$ and set $$\Phi_\ell(z)=\frac12\bigl(\mathcal{S}^+_\ell v(z)+\mathcal{S}^-_\ell v(z)\bigr).$$ This is a finite continuous function on $\mathcal B$. For all sufficiently large $N$, $\epsilon_N\le\ell$, and monotonicity of both slopes with respect to their length and <a href="#eq:dp-J-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-J-bounds">[eq:dp-J-bounds]</a> give $J_{\epsilon_N}\le\Phi_\ell$. Apply <a href="#eq:contact-stopped-expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-stopped-expectation">[eq:contact-stopped-expectation]</a> at $\tau_N$ and pass to the weak limit using this fixed continuous function and <a href="#eq:dp-J-contact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-J-contact">[eq:dp-J-contact]</a>. The result is $$\label{eq:contact-terminal-slopes}
  \int\Phi_\ell(z_{\rm end})\,d\mu\ge H_*\qquad(\ell>0).$$ Take a decreasing sequence $\ell_h\downarrow0$ with $\ell_h\le1$. The functions $\Phi_{\ell_h}$ decrease to $H_v$ pointwise, because the two slope limits agree for $v$. Their uniform upper bound on $\mathcal B$ is $U_B$ after increasing its fixed constant. Hence $U_B-\Phi_{\ell_h}(z_{\rm end})$ are nonnegative and increase to $U_B-H_v(z_{\rm end})$. Monotone convergence and <a href="#eq:contact-terminal-slopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-terminal-slopes">[eq:contact-terminal-slopes]</a> give $$\int\bigl(U_B-H_v(z_{\rm end})\bigr)\,d\mu\le U_B-H_*<\infty.$$ The integrand would be infinite where $H_v=-\infty$, proving the first claim of <a href="#eq:contact-limit-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-limit-properties">[eq:contact-limit-properties]</a>.

For the contact claim fix $j$ and let $\nu_N^j$ and $\nu^j$ be the marginals of $\mu_N$ and $\mu$ for the marked point $z_j$. They converge weakly. Suppose that $\nu^j$ gives positive mass to $\{v-W>0\}$. All these limit marks have $t\ge\delta_j$. Since $v-W$ is continuous and nonnegative for $t>0$, there is $\alpha>0$ such that the relatively open set $$U=\{(x,t)\in\mathcal B:t>\delta_j/2,\ v(x,t)-W(x,t)>\alpha\}$$ has $\nu^j(U)>0$ and its closure is contained in $\{t\ge\delta_j/2,\ v-W\ge\alpha\}$. For example, first choose $\alpha$ with positive mass where $v-W>2\alpha$. The zero-length continuity of the quadratic envelopes is uniform on this compact closure. Thus <a href="#eq:dp-contact-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-contact-gap">[eq:dp-contact-gap]</a> gives $$2\epsilon_NJ_{\epsilon_N}(z)
   \le-\frac{\alpha}{2}$$ on $U$ for all sufficiently large $N$. In particular, $J_{\epsilon_N}\le-\alpha/(4\epsilon_N)$ on $U$. By the open-set direction of Portmanteau, $\nu^j(U)\le\liminf_N\nu_N^j(U)$, so these probabilities are bounded below by a fixed positive number for large $N$. The uniform upper bound <a href="#eq:contact-J-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-J-upper">[eq:contact-J-upper]</a> on all marks and <a href="#eq:contact-stopped-expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-stopped-expectation">[eq:contact-stopped-expectation]</a> at $\sigma_{j,N}$ would then give $$J_{\epsilon_N}(z_*)\le\int J_{\epsilon_N}\,d\nu_N^j
  \le-\frac{\alpha}{4\epsilon_N}\nu_N^j(U)
      +U_B\bigl(1-\nu_N^j(U)\bigr)\longrightarrow-\infty.$$ This contradicts <a href="#eq:dp-J-contact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dp-J-contact">[eq:dp-J-contact]</a>. Thus $W(z_j)=v(z_j)$ almost surely. A countable intersection proves this simultaneously for all $j$. The derivation of <a href="#eq:contact-limit-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-limit-properties">[eq:contact-limit-properties]</a> used unconditional expectations; it does not require conditioning on the flag.

For each fixed $j$ define the subset of $\mathcal X$ $$E_j=\left\{\eta=1,\quad
      |x_{\rm end}-x_j-p_j(t_{\rm end}-\delta_j)|\le K\delta_j\right\}.$$ This event retains the actual terminal time in the prelimit laws. It is closed: the flag condition is closed and the norm is a continuous function of the terminal point, the $j$-th marked point, and $p_j$. Equation <a href="#eq:contact-finite-localization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-finite-localization">[eq:contact-finite-localization]</a> gives $\mu_N(E_j)\ge1/2$ whenever $N\ge j$. The closed-set Portmanteau inequality has the direction $$\mu(E_j)\ge\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mu_N(E_j)\ge\frac12.$$ These events need not be independent. For every $h$ their tail union has measure at least $1/2$, since it contains each $E_j$ with $j\ge h$. Continuity of probability from above therefore gives $$\mu\left(\limsup_{j\to\infty}E_j\right)
   =\lim_{h\to\infty}\mu\left(\bigcup_{j\ge h}E_j\right)\ge\frac12.$$ Choose one outcome in this limsup event and in the full-measure sets of <a href="#eq:contact-mark-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-mark-limit">[eq:contact-mark-limit]</a>–<a href="#eq:contact-limit-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-limit-properties">[eq:contact-limit-properties]</a>. Every $E_j$ includes $\eta=1$, so this outcome has $t_{\rm end}=0$ and $t_j=\delta_j$ for every $j$. It belongs to $E_j$ for an infinite set of indices, at each of which its localization inequality is $$|x_{\rm end}-x_j+p_j\delta_j|\le K\delta_j.$$ The same outcome has a finite common slope at the endpoint, contact at all the marks, and the indicated fits. Its endpoint and the infinite set of these marks establish <a href="#eq:contact-propagation-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-propagation-conclusion">[eq:contact-propagation-conclusion]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:rigidity"></a>
### Rigidity of the final limit

The propagation result produces contacts at shrinking scales near one terminal point. The finite slope at that point makes the final limit have constant forward and backward envelope slopes at every length. We first show why this forces an affine function.

<a id="stmt-lem-slope-rigidity"></a>
<div id="lem:slope-rigidity" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:constant-envelope-slopes"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Rigidity from constant envelope slopes). *Let $v\in\mathcal{A}_{m,C_0}$, with the class and envelopes defined in Section <a href="#sec:limiting-tools" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:limiting-tools">4</a>. Suppose that for some $(b,t_0)\in\mathbb{R}^m\times\mathbb{R}$ and $B\in\mathbb{R}$, $$\label{eq:constant-envelope-slopes}
  \mathcal{S}_\ell^+v(b,t_0)=\mathcal{S}_\ell^-v(b,t_0)=B
       \qquad\text{for every }\ell>0.$$ Then $v$ is affine.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-slope-rigidity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-slope-rigidity</summary>

<a id="eq:future-paraboloid"></a>
*Proof.* Translate $(b,t_0)$ to $(0,0)$ and subtract the value there; write $V$ for the resulting function. These changes preserve the class and the envelope slopes. The forward equality in <a href="#eq:constant-envelope-slopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:constant-envelope-slopes">[eq:constant-envelope-slopes]</a> gives $\mathcal{P}_tV(0,0)=Bt$ for $t>0$, and therefore $$\label{eq:future-paraboloid}
  V(y,t)\le Bt+\frac{|y|^2}{2t}\qquad(t>0).$$

<a id="eq:rigidity-optimizer-bound"></a>
Fix any $(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^m\times\mathbb{R}$. At a large length $\ell$, a maximizing position $y_\ell$ for $\mathcal{P}_\ell V(x,t)$ satisfies $$\label{eq:rigidity-optimizer-bound}
  |y_\ell|\le C_{x,t}\ell(1+\log\ell)^2.$$ This is the optimizer estimate from Section <a href="#sec:limiting-tools" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:limiting-tools">4</a>, applied on a box of size comparable to $\ell$; the translated function has the same growth estimates with constants depending on a fixed value and fit. Explicitly, if the displacement is $b_\ell\ell$ with $b_\ell\ge2$, comparison with zero displacement and the bound $|V(y,s)|\le C K(1+\log K)^2$ on boxes of size $K$ give $$b_\ell\le C_{x,t}(1+\log\ell+\log b_\ell)^2.$$ Absorbing the logarithmic term in $b_\ell$ gives $b_\ell\le C_{x,t}'(1+\log\ell)^2$, which proves <a href="#eq:rigidity-optimizer-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-optimizer-bound">[eq:rigidity-optimizer-bound]</a>.

<a id="eq:directed-comparison"></a>
For $\ell$ sufficiently large, $t+\ell>0$, so <a href="#eq:future-paraboloid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:future-paraboloid">[eq:future-paraboloid]</a> applies at the optimizer. The difference between the two quadratic costs is $$\begin{aligned}
  \frac{|y_\ell|^2}{2(t+\ell)}-\frac{|y_\ell-x|^2}{2\ell}
  &=-\frac{t|y_\ell|^2}{2\ell(t+\ell)}
       +\frac{x\cdot y_\ell}{\ell}-\frac{|x|^2}{2\ell}\\
  &=O_{x,t}\bigl((1+\log\ell)^4\bigr)=o(\ell)
\end{aligned}$$ by <a href="#eq:rigidity-optimizer-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-optimizer-bound">[eq:rigidity-optimizer-bound]</a>. Consequently $$\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\mathcal{S}_\ell^+V(x,t)\le B.$$ The forward slopes are nondecreasing in $\ell$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:envelope-slopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:envelope-slopes">17</a>. Thus $\mathcal{S}_\ell^+V(x,t)\le B$ for every $\ell>0$. Evaluating the envelope at any competitor gives the global directed comparison $$\label{eq:directed-comparison}
  V(y,t+\ell)-V(x,t)\le B\ell+\frac{|y-x|^2}{2\ell}
       \qquad(x,y\in\mathbb{R}^m,\ t\in\mathbb{R},\ \ell>0).$$

The envelopes are attained by Lemma <a href="#lem:quadratic-envelopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:quadratic-envelopes">16</a>. At the origin, <a href="#eq:constant-envelope-slopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:constant-envelope-slopes">[eq:constant-envelope-slopes]</a> therefore supplies, for every $\ell,r>0$, a forward equality point $(\ell q_+,\ell)$ and a backward equality point $(-r q_-,-r)$, with $$V(\ell q_+,\ell)=B\ell+\tfrac12\ell|q_+|^2,
  \qquad V(-r q_-,-r)=-Br-\tfrac12r|q_-|^2.$$ Apply <a href="#eq:directed-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:directed-comparison">[eq:directed-comparison]</a> directly from the backward point to the forward point. After canceling $B(\ell+r)$, it gives $$0\ge \frac{\ell|q_+|^2+r|q_-|^2}{2}
       -\frac{|\ell q_++r q_-|^2}{2(\ell+r)}
    =\frac{\ell r}{2(\ell+r)}|q_+-q_-|^2.$$ Hence every forward and backward equality velocity is one vector $q$, independent of all lengths and choices. We have $$V(qs,s)=(B+|q|^2/2)s\qquad(s\in\mathbb{R}).$$ Let $A(x,t)=q\cdot x+(B-|q|^2/2)t$. Comparing $(x,t)$ to a point $(qs,s)$ on this line in <a href="#eq:directed-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:directed-comparison">[eq:directed-comparison]</a> yields $$V(x,t)\le A(x,t)+\frac{|x-qt|^2}{2(t-s)}\quad(s<t),
  \qquad
  V(x,t)\ge A(x,t)-\frac{|x-qt|^2}{2(s-t)}\quad(s>t).$$ Letting $s\to-\infty$ and $s\to+\infty$ proves $V=A$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-slope-rigidity-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-slope-rigidity</summary>

<a id="eq:final-contact-data"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:liouville" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:liouville">8</a>.* Suppose that the class contains a nonaffine function. Proposition <a href="#prop:envelope-contact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:envelope-contact">22</a> supplies a centered solution $v$, a number $\kappa>0$, and its subsolution envelope $$W(x,t)=\mathcal{C}_t v(x,t)-\kappa^2t/2\qquad(t>0)$$ with the stated contact and finite-slope properties. Proposition <a href="#prop:contact-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:contact-propagation">27</a> supplies a point $x_{\mathrm{end}}$, infinitely many indices $j$ with $r_j=2^{-j}$, contacts $(x_j,r_j)$, and fits $(p_j,a_j)$ there of radius $r_j$, such that $$\label{eq:final-contact-data}
  H_v(x_{\mathrm{end}},0)>-\infty,\qquad
  W(x_j,r_j)=v(x_j,r_j),\qquad
  |x_{\mathrm{end}}-x_j+p_jr_j|\le K r_j$$ for one finite $K$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:fitted-frame-covariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fitted-frame-covariance">25</a> at each of these marks, and denote the transformed functions by $\bar v_j$. Their images of $(x_{\mathrm{end}},0)$ are $(b_j,0)$, where $$b_j=\frac{x_{\mathrm{end}}-x_j+p_jr_j}{r_j}.$$ These vectors are bounded by <a href="#eq:final-contact-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-contact-data">[eq:final-contact-data]</a>. Every $\bar v_j$ has value zero and the zero fit of radius one at $(0,1)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:fit-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fit-compactness">15</a> gives a subsequence for which $\bar v_j\to v_\infty$ locally uniformly and $b_j\to b$. The uniform growth bounds also give convergence of the forward and backward envelopes at each fixed positive length, by Lemma <a href="#lem:quadratic-envelopes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:quadratic-envelopes">16</a>.

The slope covariance <a href="#eq:fitted-slope-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fitted-slope-covariance">[eq:fitted-slope-covariance]</a> gives $$\mathcal{S}_\ell^\pm\bar v_j(b_j,0)
    =\mathcal{S}_{r_j\ell}^\pm v(x_{\mathrm{end}},0)-a_j
       \qquad(\ell>0).$$ Let $B=\mathcal{S}_1^+v_\infty(b,0)$, which is finite. For each fixed $\ell>0$, subtract the equality with the forward slope at length one: $$\begin{aligned}
  \mathcal{S}_\ell^\pm\bar v_j(b_j,0)-\mathcal{S}_1^+\bar v_j(b_j,0)
   &=\mathcal{S}_{r_j\ell}^\pm v(x_{\mathrm{end}},0)
        -\mathcal{S}_{r_j}^+v(x_{\mathrm{end}},0)\\
   &\longrightarrow0.
\end{aligned}$$ The limit is zero because both unshifted slopes tend to the same finite value $H_v(x_{\mathrm{end}},0)$. Passing to the limit on the left gives $$\mathcal{S}_\ell^+v_\infty(b,0)=\mathcal{S}_\ell^-v_\infty(b,0)=B
       \qquad(\ell>0).$$ This argument uses each fixed length along the same locally convergent subsequence; no convergence of the individual shifts $a_j$ is required. Lemma <a href="#lem:slope-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:slope-rigidity">28</a> now makes $v_\infty$ affine.

The contacts retain a positive envelope gap. By <a href="#eq:contact-barycentric-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-barycentric-gap">[eq:contact-barycentric-gap]</a> at the contacts in <a href="#eq:final-contact-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-contact-data">[eq:final-contact-data]</a> and the gap covariance <a href="#eq:contact-W-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-W-covariance">[eq:contact-W-covariance]</a>, $$\mathcal{C}_1\bar v_j(0,1)-\bar v_j(0,1)=\kappa^2/2.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:barycentric-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:barycentric-envelope">19</a> passes this identity to the locally uniform limit with its uniform growth bound. Thus $$\mathcal{C}_1v_\infty(0,1)-v_\infty(0,1)=\kappa^2/2>0.$$ For an affine function, the average of its values at any configuration is its value at the barycenter, so the nonnegative variance penalty makes $\mathcal{C}_1v_\infty=v_\infty$. This contradiction proves Theorem <a href="#thm:liouville" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:liouville">8</a>. The reduction in Section <a href="#sec:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reduction">3</a> then proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

      "caption": "From thin cylinders to sheared fitting domains. The left panel shows a two-dimensional section of $Z_{λ}(z,r;P)$, with axial radius $r$ and transverse radius $\\sqrt\\lambda\\,r$; the dotted line indicates the base direction used for rescaling. Stretching transverse directions makes the small inclination of a nearby fitted axis visible as a finite shear. Fixed margins give contained sheared boxes, and passage to the limit yields $Q_{R}(z;p)$, shown on the right in increment coordinates $(h,ℓ)$. The panels are schematic: the finite-scale cylinder image is not asserted to equal the limiting box.",

      "witness_description": "Two schematic panels showing domain transformations. Left panel: titled \"physical cylinder\" at top and \"$Z_\\lambda(z, r; P)$\" at bottom; depicts a tilted two-dimensional cylinder/rectangle cross-section with center point $z$, axial radius $r$ indicated by a dimension line along the side, transverse radius $\\sqrt{\\lambda} r$ indicated at the bottom, and an axis marked by a dotted line with an arrow labeled $P$ and \"base direction\". Between panels: two horizontal arrows pointing right, the upper labeled \"stretch transverse directions\", the lower labeled \"take fixed margins, then pass to the limit\". Right panel: titled \"limiting sheared box\"; depicts a Cartesian plane with horizontal axis $h$ and vertical axis $\\ell$ centered at 0; a sheared parallelogram symmetric about the origin along the dashed line $h = p\\ell$; vertical half-height $R$ marked on the $\\ell$-axis, horizontal width $R$ indicated with an arrow; below the panel is the inequality \"$|\\ell| \\le R, \\quad |h - p\\ell| \\le R$\"."

      "caption": "Schematic localization at two selected scales, shown in one spatial direction with different horizontal and vertical units. Their time-zero sections both contain the same point $(x_{\\rm end},0)$, expressing $|x_{\\rm end}-x_j+p_j\\delta_j|\\le K\\delta_j$. (ka) at (4.00,1.68)\n      {$x=x_k+p_k(t-\\delta_k)$};\n  \\draw[fitorange!75] (ka.west)--(3.42,1.68)--(-0.72,1.13);\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left,text width=4.25cm] at (4.00,0.46)\n (0.75,-0.64)--(0.75,-0.82)\n                        (2.50,-0.64)--(2.50,-0.82);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A 2D diagram with horizontal spatial axis $x$ and vertical time axis $t$, intersecting at 0. Two points $(x_j, \\delta_j)$ and $(x_k, \\delta_k)$ are plotted at heights $\\delta_j$ and $\\delta_k$ ($\\delta_j > \\delta_k$). Through $(x_j, \\delta_j)$, a dashed line $x = x_j + p_j(t - \\delta_j)$ runs within a wider shaded strip of half-width $K\\delta_j$ marked along the bottom horizontal axis. Through $(x_k, \\delta_k)$, a dashed line $x = x_k + p_k(t - \\delta_k)$ runs within a narrower shaded strip of half-width $K\\delta_k$. Both shaded strips overlap at the $t=0$ boundary at a common point labeled $(x_{\\mathrm{end}}, 0)$. Annotations to the right show: equations of the two dashed lines, condition \"$j, k \\in \\mathcal{J}, \\; j < k.$\", and text \"One endpoint lies in both strips.\""
